3.2.4   Responses of leaf elongation rate and transpiration to vapour- [SOURCE: Page_63]

pressure deficit [SOURCE: Page_63]

The responses of LER and transpiration to leaf–air VPD were calculated du- ring the day in scenario 1 only, with LER being measured as described above. Transpiration rate (TR) was calculated from the weight loss of each pot divided by the total leaf (sheath + blade) area. Direct evaporation from the soil was es- timated by measuring the weight loss of pots containing artificial plants, which were watered to maintain a soil water potential similar to that of the pots contai- ning real live plants. The weight of each pot was measured every minute, and then the average was calculated and stored every 15 min. The LER of the youn- gest growing leaf measured at the 15 min intervals was first expressed with units of thermal time in order to remove the effects of temperature. It was then avera- ged over the period of stabilized VPD (i.e. the last 4 h of the 6-h period) and then normalized by the mean TR during those 4 h to remove the effects of plant size and leaf area. The normalized LER and TR data were then fitted with a linear mo- del constrained to 1 at the reference VPD, so that only one parameter described each response, the sensitivities to VPD of LER (sLER, kPa -1 ) and TR (sTR, kPa -1 ). [SOURCE: Page_63]

3.2.5   Traits linked to rates of development, leaf expansion, and trans- [SOURCE: Page_63]

piration [SOURCE: Page_63]

The progress of plant development on the main stem was determined from the leaf stage (LS), adapted from Haun (1973) : [SOURCE: Page_63]

l LS = n +                                    (3.2) L [SOURCE: Page_63]

where n is the number of ligulate leaves, l is the exposed length of leaf n+1 [SOURCE: Page_63]

at the time of measurement, and L is the final length of the blade of leaf n+1. The exposed length of a leaf was measured with a ruler as the distance from leaf tip to the upper collar of the sheath tube. LS was determined on all plants in scenario 1 from the day before to the last of the LER measurements. The rate of leaf appearance (LAR, leaves ◦ Cd−1 ; inverse of the phyllochron) was calculated as the slope of the relationship between LS and thermal time (base 0 °C). [SOURCE: Page_64]

Because the elongation of leaf 5 was measured during at least one night for all the accessions, the absolute LER of all accessions was compared based on the LER of leaf 5 (LERm, mmh−1 ) measured at 20 °C, 2 - 3 d after leaf appearance (i.e. when LER was at its maximum value). The transpiration rate at 20 °C and VPD=1.3 kPa was calculated as the mean of absolute transpiration rate as measured on each of the four nights during the 4-h period of stabilised VPD. At the end of the 4-d period, the whole-plant leaf area was measured (see below). The transpiration rate per unit leaf area (TR, gcm−2 h−1 ) was then calculated by first deducting the evaporation recorded in pots with artificial plants, and then dividing by the whole-plant leaf area. [SOURCE: Page_64]

### 3.2. Materials and methods                                                       63

3.2.6   Traits linked to plant structure [SOURCE: Page_65]

All plant structural traits were measured after the fourth day of LER and TR measurements. The total number of leaves per plant (TL, lea f plant−1 ) was coun- ted. The leaf blades and sheaths of the main stem and tillers were then separated and their lengths, widths, and surface areas were measured using an electronic planimeter (LI-3100C, LI-COR) and used to calculate various traits linked to plant structure. The total leaf area per plant was calculated (LAT, cm^2 plant−1 ). The length-to-width ratio (rlw^2, dimensionless) and surface area (LA$_2$, cm^2 plant−1 ) of the blade of main-stem leaf 2 were calculated as proxies of early plant vigour (Rebetzke et al., 2004). It was correlated to seed size (Supplementary Fig. S$_3$). We also determined the slope of the relationship between main-stem individual leaf- blade surface area and leaf rank normalized by the surface area of the blade of leaf 2 (sLAK, dimensionless), and the ratio of leaf-blade to total leaf (sheath + blade) surface area (rLA, dimensionless). [SOURCE: Page_65]

The main-stem leaf blades and sheaths of each plant were pooled and oven- dried at 85 °C until they reached constant mass. The dry mass of the samples was measured and specific leaf mass (mass per leaf area, MLA ; gDMcm−2 lea f ) was calculated by dividing total plant dry mass by the total leaf area LAT. The samples were then milled and their total N concentration (N mass per unit dry mass) was determined using the Dumas combustion method (Horwitz, 1980) using a FlashEA 1112 N/Protein Analyzer (ThermoFisher Scientific) and the mass of N per plant was calculated (PN, gN plant−1 ). Specific leaf N (SLN, gNm−2 lea f ) was calculated by dividing PN by the total leaf area of the plant. [SOURCE: Page_65]

subsectionData analyses All data analyses were carried out using the R soft- ware (www.r-project.org). ANOVAs on each variable were performed with the function aov, considering species as factors and genotypes as replicates. Confi- dence intervals of differences between means of pairs of species were calculated using a Tukey test (R function TukeyHSD) and the significance of these diffe- rences were calculated with the function HSD.test with α = 0.05 . The procedure [SOURCE: Page_65]

Chapitre 3. Differential sensitivity to temperature and evaporative demand in 64                                                                wheat relatives for building correlograms consisted of the following steps : (i) normality Sha- piro–Wilk test ; (ii) calculation of Spearman correlations ; and (iii) calculation of q- values (P-value corrected for false-discovery rate). Significant correlations were considered at q < 0.01. To compare the intra- versus interspecific variability of each trait, we calculated the ratio of the corresponding coefficient of variation of standard deviation. Principal component analyses (PCAs) were performed using the function PCA of the FactorMineR R package (Lê et al., 2008). We considered several sets of traits (e.g. all traits, or traits linked to sensitivities to temperature and VPD only), with data at the accession level. Values of each species on the first five axes of the PCA were determined by calculating the barycentre of all acces- sions in each species. A hierarchical clustering on principal components (HCPC) analysis of species was then performed based on these first five axes of the PCA using the HCPC function of the FactorMineR package. In the case of the analysis of the set of traits comprising only the five traits linked to sensitivities, and be- cause the phenotypic space was then automatically reduced to five dimensions (five variables), the hierarchical clustering analysis was carried out based on these five traits rather than on principal components of the PCA. Approximately unbia- sed (AU) P-values (%) were calculated using the pvclust function of the pv.clust R package, with 1000 bootstrap replications. [SOURCE: Page_66]

3.3     Results [SOURCE: Page_66]

3.3.1    Interspecific variability of traits related to plant structure and [SOURCE: Page_66]

rates of development, leaf expansion, and transpiration, and [SOURCE: Page_66]

their response to temperature and VPD [SOURCE: Page_66]

We measured nine traits related to plant structure and three traits related to the rate of plant development and growth in 60 accessions covering a wide gene- tic diversity in 12 groups (five accessions in each group) of wheat-related species and subspecies (Fig. 3.1). Traits related to the rates of plant development and growth (LAR, LERm) and plant size (LA2, LAT, and PN) showed a high variabi- [SOURCE: Page_66]

### 3.3. Results                                                                        65

lity and significant differences between species (Fig. 3.3, see Supplementary Table S$_3$ for results of ANOVA and Supplementary Table S$_4$ for comparing intra- vs in- terspecific variations). Among these traits, LAR increased with ploidy level (Fig. 3.3L, P<0.001), while LA$_2$, LAT, and PN were higher for the diploid species than for the polyploid species (2n > 4n > 6n ; Fig. 3.3A, D, H ; all P<0.001). Traits linked to plant structure but not expected to be linked to plant size (i.e. rlw^2, rLA, and sLAK) showed no clear patterns with ploidy (Fig. 3.3B, F, G), and the other traits (TL, SLN, and TR) also showed no clear pattern. MLA and SLN both showed low overall variability compared to the other traits that we measured. [SOURCE: Page_67]

We examined the sensitivities of LER to temperature and VPD in the 60 ac- cessions. Each night, a temperature between 4 °C and 36 °C was applied for 6 h together with a period of 6 h at the reference temperature of 20 °C (Supple- mentary Fig. S$_1$). An example for T. turgidum subsp. durum cultivar Brumaire is shown in Fig. 3.2, where temperature was first maintained at 20 °C for 6 h and then increased to 24 °C. The LER oscillated around 4 mm h-1 during the first period and then increased rapidly to ≈ 5mm−1 when temperature was increa- sed (Fig. 3.2C). We normalized the LER measured at the tested temperature by that measured at the reference temperature on the same night in order to exa- mine the effect of the temperature change regardless of differences in LER due to differences in leaf rank or time since leaf emergence (Fig. 3.4A). We fitted the data to three different function equations to estimate the cardinal temperatures of LER, namely a beta function (Wang and Engel, 1998), an Arrhenius function (Parent and Tardieu, 2012), and a segmented linear equation (Eqn. 3.1 ). For all accessions, the segmented linear equation provided the best fit (Supplementary Fig. S$_2$, Supplementary Table S$_5$), and all data were therefore analysed using this model (Fig. 3.4A provides an example of the type of response we obtained). [SOURCE: Page_67]

Chapitre 3. Differential sensitivity to temperature and evaporative demand in 68                                                                wheat relatives plementary Table S$_7$ for values at the species level). There was no clear pattern between ploidy levels, genomes, or between wild versus domesticated species. [SOURCE: Page_70]

We quantified the responses of LER and TR to VPD during the day using an approach similar to that described above for the response of LER to temperature during the night. Each day, a VPD between 0.5 kPa and 3.0 kPa was applied for 6 h together with a period of 6 h at the reference VPD of 1.3 kPa . Examples of time courses we obtained for LER and Transpiration are shown in Fig. 3.2C, D. LER os- cillated around 5.5 mmh−1 at the reference VPD and decreased to 4mmh − 1 when VPD was increased to 2.7 kPa. At the same time, transpiration rate, which stabi- lized at 13gh − 1 at the reference VPD, increased to 19gh − 1. LER and TR (plant transpiration rate per area) at the tested values of VPD were normalized by their corresponding value at the reference VPD to remove the effects of leaf rank, time since leaf emergence, and plant leaf area. Both responses to VPD were fitted with a linear model, the slope of which is the sensitivity of LER (sLER) or TR (sTR) to VPD (Fig. 3.4B, C). sLER showed significant variability between species and sub- species (Fig. 3.4G), ranging from ˘0.268kPa˘1 to ˘0.037kPa˘1, while sTR (Fig.3.4H) showed lower variability (Supplementary Table S$_6$ for values at the accession le- vel and Supplementary Table S$_7$ for values at the species level). However, at the level of single traits, we did not observe any clear patterns between the 12 groups of species. [SOURCE: Page_70]

3.3.2    Ploidy level, phylogeny, and breeding together explain the phe- [SOURCE: Page_70]

notypic space of all traits [SOURCE: Page_70]

An understanding of the structure of the interspecific diversity and how the intraspecific diversity is nested inside is made possible by considering the multi- dimensional space constituted by all traits together (the ‘phenotypic space’). We found significant correlations between most traits (Fig. 3.5, Supplementary Table S8). The strongest positive correlations were between traits related to plant struc- ture and plant size, but we also found significant correlations between traits of different types, for example between response traits and traits related to plant [SOURCE: Page_70]

### 3.3. Results                                                                       69

structure. [SOURCE: Page_71]

We used principal component analyses (PCAs) to reduce the number of di- mensions of the phenotypic space to five. PCA based on all traits at the accession level (Fig. 3.6A–D) showed that within each group of species, the diversity of va- lues for the first three components was low compared to the overall diversity ob- served at the interspecific level (Fig. 3.6B, D). The first principal component (PC$_1$) explained 30% of the observed variance and was mainly explained by traits rela- ted to development rate (e.g. LAR and LERm) and traits linked to plant size (e.g. LAT and TL ; Fig. 3.6A), and it discriminated diploid species from tetraploid and hexaploid species (fig. 3.6B). Diploid species had a smaller leaf 2 area (LA$_2$), lo- wer leaf appearance rate (LAR), fewer leaves (TL), and a lower total leaf area per plant (LAT) compared with tetraploid and hexaploid species (Fig. 3.3). However, because most of the wild species used in this study were diploids, and because T. turgidum subsp. dicoccoides was close to the origin of PC$_1$, we are not able to conclude if this axis discriminated species based on domestication or on ploidy level. [SOURCE: Page_71]

The hierarchical clustering on principal components (HCPC) analysis based on the first five components of the PCA classified the 12 groups into six clusters (fig. 3.6E). The first branching of the dendogram (corresponding to the highest relative distance) separated the diploid and polyploid species. For the diploids, the second order of the dendrogram separated genome A from the other two diploid genomes, whilst for the polyploids it separated T. turgidum subsp. dicoc- coides and subsp. dicoccum from the other polyploid species. The third order of clustering grouped together T. turgidum subsp. durum elite cultivars and the two hexaploid species (T. aestivum subsp. aestivum and subsp. spelta), while T. turgi- dum subsp. durum landraces were grouped with T. turgidum subsp. turgidum. The wild and cultivated emmer wheat species (T. turgidum subsp. dicoccoides and subsp. dicoccum) were grouped together. Thus, the phenotypic space based on the 17 traits that we studied discriminated wheat-related species and subspe- cies based on their ploidy level, domestication, and breeding, and the resulting [SOURCE: Page_71]

3.3.3    Phylogeny structures the phenotypic space of traits related to [SOURCE: Page_74]

the rate of plant development and growth, and their responses [SOURCE: Page_74]

to temperature and VPD [SOURCE: Page_74]

We conducted a similar analysis combining PCA and HCPC analysis but ba- sed on traits linked to plant structure only (Supplementary Fig. S$_4$) and obtained similar results to when all 17 traits were used (with marginal differences), sug- gesting that the structure traits drove the clustering of the 12 groups of species. We therefore performed a similar analysis on traits linked to rates and sensitivi- ties whilst excluding structure traits (Fig. 3.7). The first axis discriminated species with high LAR from those with low TR, sLER, and Tmax, while the second axis discriminated species with high Tmax from those with low Tmin, Topt, and sTR. Topt was negatively correlated with LERm (Supplementary Table S$_8$) and these were the main contributing traits to the third axis. The resulting HCPC analysis first segregated two groups (Fig. 3.7E). The first consisted of the cultivated tetra- ploid and hexaploid species, as well as Ae. tauschii, while the second consisted of the wild and cultivated diploids and the wild tetraploid species T. turgidum subsp. dicoccoides. Within these two groups, the HCPC analysis produced a few unexpected results, with T. turgidum subsp. dicoccoides clustered with T. mo- nococcum (subsp. monococcum and subsp. beoticum), T. aestivum subsp. spelta clustered with a group of three T. turgidum subspecies, and Ae. tauschii in the same cluster as T. aestivum subsp. aestivum. Overall, these results indicated that the rate of development of growth and the sensitivities to temperature and VPD are well-conserved, with low variability within species compared to the phenoty- pic space of wheat-related species, and that their interspecific variability is mainly structured by phylogeny. [SOURCE: Page_74]

Finally, we analysed the structure of the phenotypic space of response traits [SOURCE: Page_74]

Chapitre 3. Differential sensitivity to temperature and evaporative demand in 74                                                                wheat relatives only, knowing that the values of individual traits did not show any clear trends (Fig. 3.4). Because we only measured five response traits, the phenotypic space was automatically reduced to five dimensions (five variables), which appeared as independent in the PCA (Fig. 3.8A), we then performed a hierarchical cluste- ring analysis based on these five traits. The resulting dendogram again showed clear patterns (Fig. 3.8E). The clustering first segregated two wild diploid species, Ae. speltoïdes and T. uratu, from the other species, indicating that they had signi- ficantly different responses to temperature and evaporative demand. Apart from this, the analysis clustered three other groups, the first of which comprised all tetraploid species, regardless of wild/domesticated subgroups, the second group contained the two wild and domesticated T. monococcum species (subsp. mo- nococcum and boeticumm, respectively), and the third group contained the two hexaploid species (T. aestivum subsp. spelta and subsp. aestivum) and Ae. tau- schii, that is, all species with a D genome. This analysis therefore indicated that the interspecific variability in temperature and VPD responses was structured by both the ploidy level and the phylogenetic relationships. However, domestication and/or breeding pressure does not appear to have played a role in modifying these responses.. [SOURCE: Page_76]

Chapitre 3. Differential sensitivity to temperature and evaporative demand in 76                                                                wheat relatives 3.4     Discussion [SOURCE: Page_78]

3.4.1   Modelling the sensitivities of leaf elongation and transpiration [SOURCE: Page_78]

rates to temperature and vapour pressure deficit for a large ge- [SOURCE: Page_78]

netic diversity [SOURCE: Page_78]

The responses of expansion processes of plant organs to temperature have been studied extensively (reviewed in Parent et al., 2012). Most of these studies have concluded that the response is similar to that of plant development, and the few studies that have assessed intraspecific variability have suggested that it is low (Parent and Tardieu, 2012). The result is that most crop models use si- milar formalisms and parameter values for both the response of development and expansion processes without including genetic variability in these parame- ters (Parent and Tardieu, 2014). A range of formalisms are used in crop models, from the simplest linear model to more complex, curvilinear models (Kumudini et al., 2014 ; Parent and Tardieu, 2014 ; Wang et al., 2017). In this study, we used a segmented linear formalism, which provided an adequate fit to the observed sen- sitivities in 60 accessions of wheat-related species (Supplementary Fig. S$_2$, Sup- plementary Table S$_6$). As expected, the observed intraspecific variability was low (Supplementary Tables S$_4$ and S$_6$), and so was the interspecific variability (albeit not zero ; see below, Fig. 3.4 ; Supplementary Table S$_4$). This indicates that a mo- delling strategy based on parsimony with a single set of parameter values for all wheat-related species is probably a good strategy (Parent et al., 2016). Such a model with no genotypic variation would be probably very close to one that assumes organized genetic variation, but would have the advantage of avoiding time-consuming experiments on each individual wheat accession. [SOURCE: Page_78]

To our knowledge, there have been no studies to date that have assessed the response of leaf elongation to VPD in wheat. This is despite the fact that most cereal species react strongly to changes in evaporative demand, and large genetic variability in the response of LER to VPD has been found in the species in which [SOURCE: Page_78]

### 3.4. Discussion                                                                    77

it has been studied (e.g. Parent et al., 2010b ; Welcker et al., 2011). Here, we have demonstrated that a linear model can fit this response across a large diversity of wheat-related species. The range of sensitivities at the interspecific level (from –10% to –25% kPa–1 ; Fig. 3.4G) was very close to that observed in maize (Welcker et al., 2011 ; Lacube et al., 2017). Given that consideration of this response can greatly improve prediction of leaf area in contrasting field sites for maize, we would also expect it to be the case in wheat growth predictions. [SOURCE: Page_79]

Although the response of transpiration to VPD has been investigated in many species including bread wheat (e.g. Schoppach and Sadok, 2012 ; Schoppach et al., 2012), and very high variability has been found across generations of selections of Australian wheat (Schoppach et al., 2016), there has been no information about its variability in wheat-relative species. Unexpectedly, the variability of responses observed in this study was very low at both the intra- and interspecific level com- pared to that found in 23 cultivars released from 1890 to 2010 in Australia (Fig. 3.4H ; Supplementary Tables S$_4$ and S$_6$ ; Schoppach et al., 2016). For some geno- types, Schoppach et al. (2012) observed a break-point in the TR–VPD relationship between 2.4 kPa and 3.9 kPa, whereas in the range of VPDs that we tested a li- near model fitted the response of all accessions without any break-point (Fig. 3.4C). This could be explained by the limited range of VPD used here (0.5–3.0 kPa compared with 0.8–4.5 kPa in Schoppach et al., 2012), which corresponded to the environmental range observed in the spring in most temperate regions. [SOURCE: Page_79]

3.4.2   Most traits have probably evolved slowly, following polyploidi- [SOURCE: Page_79]

zation, phylogeny, and breeding pressure [SOURCE: Page_79]

A striking result of this study was the fact that a hierarchical clustering of species based on traits that have not been directly selected was able to reproduce almost perfectly the known phylogeny of wheat-related species and subspecies. Our hierarchical clustering based on traits linked to plant structure discriminated diploid from polyploid species, regardless of domestication (e.g. T. mocococcum subsp. mocococcum and subsp. boeoticum clustered together, and T. turgidum [SOURCE: Page_79]

Chapitre 3. Differential sensitivity to temperature and evaporative demand in 78                                                                wheat relatives subsp. dicoccoides and subsp. dicoccum clustered together ; fig. 3.6 and Supple- mentary Fig. S$_4$). This suggests a specific effect of ploidy on plant physiology, such as that observed in trees (De Baerdemaeker et al., 2018). In addition, we found variations depending on the genome, with different sets of trait values between diploid species with genome A, D, or S (Fig. 3.1, fig. 3.6 and Supple- mentary Fig. S$_4$. Finally, selection pressure seems to have affected these traits (but much less than phylogeny and ploidy), with different clusters of trait values for the earliest tetraploid species (T. turgidum subsp. dicoccoides and subsp. di- coccum) and landraces of T. turgidum subsp. durum selected before the Green Revolution, compared with durum wheat cultivars released later (Fig.6 and Sup- plementary Fig. S$_4$) At the level of individual traits, the values of the five traits related to sensitivities to temperature and VPD did not indicate any clear pat- terns, with no obvious impact of selection pressure, domestication, genome or sub-genome, ploidy, or phylogenetic links between species (Fig. 3.4). In contrast, a clustering based on these traits did show clear patterns, with an effect of both ploidy and phylogenetic relationships (Fig. 3.8). Overall, species that were phy- logenetically close showed similar phenotypic spaces, indicating that these traits are well-conserved and have probably evolved slowly, following phylogeny, as has been proposed previously for the response to temperature in a much wider genetic diversity of cultivated plants (Parent and Tardieu, 2012). [SOURCE: Page_80]

3.4.3   What sources of genetic improvement are available within spe- [SOURCE: Page_80]

cies and between related species? [SOURCE: Page_80]

In addition to providing formalisms and parameter values for process-based models, we also hypothesized that our study could identify new sources of ge- netic diversity for the responses of LER and TR to VPD and temperature within wheat-related species, in particular in wild or cultivated T. turgidum or diploid species, which could then potentially enrich the genetic diversity of cultivated wheat via the use of a synthetic-wheat strategy. [SOURCE: Page_80]

Most synthetic-derived wheat cultivars have been developed by crossing Ae. [SOURCE: Page_80]

### 3.4. Discussion                                                                    79

tauschii with elite durum wheat cultivars, and more recently with T. dicoccoides, T. dicoccum, or T. turgidum (Singh and Trethowan, 2008 ; Li et al., 2018 ; Rosyara et al., 2019). Several studies have reported significant improvements in heat and drought tolerance of bread wheat cultivars derived from Ae. tauschii × T. durum crosses compared with locally adapted elite cultivars (e.g. Lopes and Reynolds, 2011 ; Mariano Cossani and Reynolds, 2015). However, the interspecific variabi- lity of wild and cultivated wheat-related species for traits related to temperature and evaporative demand has not yet been explored. [SOURCE: Page_81]

As expected, our results showed that the genetic variability of structural traits observed within wheat ancestors or the secondary gene pool was very different from that observed in modern durum or bread wheat cultivars, and mostly pre- sented lower values (Fig. 3.3), which would not be advantageous in an agrono- mic context. A source of enrichment of genetic diversity for these traits therefore needs to be sought in other wild species. In contrast, for response traits, some wild relative species or wheat ancestors might provide adaptive value if introdu- ced into cultivated wheat species (Figs 3.4 and 3.4). [SOURCE: Page_81]

The responses of LER and TR to temperature or VPD in Ae. tauschii and T. dicoccum were similar to those of T. aestivum (Figs 3.4 and 3.4). In particular, ac- cessions of Ae. tauschii presented very similar trait values compared to modern wheat lines and they delimitated a phenotypic space that was almost nested in that of T. aestivum (Fig. 3.8E). In contrast, T. uratu and Ae. speltoïdes showed very distinct responses and had higher Tmax and lower sLER than elite bread and durum wheat cultivars (Fig. 3.4). Synthetic tetraploid and hexaploid lines have been created with T. urartu and Ae. speltoïdes but, to our knowledge, they have not been evaluated for their responses to temperature or evaporative de- mand (Valkoun, 2001 ; Kishii, 2019). Although introgressions from these species within wheat is practically and methodically challenging, recent developments of genomic resources and biotechnology have good potential to accelerate the use of diploid wheat-related species other than Ae. tauschii (Brunazzi et al., 2018). Several studies have focused on the responses of vegetative and/or reproductive [SOURCE: Page_81]

Chapitre 3. Differential sensitivity to temperature and evaporative demand in 80                                                                wheat relatives traits to high temperature in Aegilops species, and genetic variability at both the intra- and interspecific levels has been observed (Pradhan et al., 2012). Although we considered only a very limited number of accessions in each species, our re- sults suggest that T. urartu and Ae. speltoïdes could be interesting sources of genes to develop wheat cultivars that are able to maintain high LER under high temperature and evaporative demand. [SOURCE: Page_82]

3.4.4   Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_82]

In addition to providing new formalisms for trait responses together with model parameters for 12 wheat-related species and subspecies, this study indi- cates that traits linked to the responses of LER and TR to temperature and VPD are well-conserved, with an unexpected low genetic variability within species. Our results suggest that vegetative traits have probably evolved slowly, follo- wing phylogenetic links between accessions, and that both domestication and phylogenetic relationships have driven the slow evolution of temperature and VPD responses. Finally, the study has identified potential sources for varietal im- provement that currently reside in wheat-related species. [SOURCE: Page_82]

## CHAPITRE 4

Analysis of the phenotypic space of sensitivities of grain [SOURCE: Page_89]

yield and its components to water and nitrogen limitations [SOURCE: Page_89]

for durum wheat and bread wheat : constitution of [SOURCE: Page_89]

sub-panels for ecophysiological studies [SOURCE: Page_89]

Résumé [SOURCE: Page_89]

Dans le contexte du changement climatique et de politiques publiques, pour une agriculture plus durable utilisant moins d’intrants, le rendement risque d’être de plus en plus affecté par des contraintes abiotiques telles que les limitations en eau et en azote. En fonction de chaque scénario environnemental spécifique, les composantes du rendement peuvent être affectées différemment par ces contraintes, car leurs déterminismes physiologiques ont leur propre diversité génétique. De plus, il existe de nombreux compromis entre ces variables, souvent liés à la com- pétition pour les ressources, ce qui limite l’espace phénotypique des réponses aux stress abiotiques. Dans cette étude, nous proposons une méthode visant à sélec- [SOURCE: Page_89]

87 [SOURCE: Page_89]

88         Chapitre 4. Sensitivities of grain yield to water and nitrogen limitation [SOURCE: Page_90]

tionner des génotypes sensibles et tolérants pour le rendement, couvrant l’espace phénotypique original des réponses de ses composantes. Nous avons analysé la sensibilité aux déficits hydrique et en azote, et l’espace phénotypique qui en ré- sulte, de deux panels de diversité de 250 génotypes de blé tendre et 200 génotypes de blé dur, cultivés respectivement dans sept et trois essais au champ compre- nant des conditions optimales et des conditions limitantes en azote et/ou eau. Nous avons d’abord étudié comment les réponses du rendement et de ses com- posantes variaient, observé que les réponses de ces composantes aux limitations hydriques et azotées étaient elles-mêmes fortement dépendantes de l’interaction avec les autres covariables expérimentales (site x année). Nous avons observé que l’espace phénotypique de la sensibilité du rendement et de ses composantes aux limitations hydriques et azotées était fortement contraint, indiquant la nécessité de le prendre en compte pour la sélection de sous-panels de diversité. Nous dé- crivons une méthode de sélection de sous-panels permettant d’explorer l’espace phénotypique initial dans les analyses écophysiologiques. Enfin, en utilisant une sélection aléatoire du nombre de sites, nous avons questionné l’importance du nombre de sites et leur impact respectif dans la sélection des sous-panels. Outre l’amélioration de nos connaissances sur les relations entre la sensibilité des com- posantes du rendement aux multiples contraintes, cette étude présente également un intérêt opérationnel pour la sélection de sous-panels respectant l’espace phé- notypique de la sensibilité du rendement aux contraintes abiotiques des panels génétiques de départ, utilisables pour des études d’écophysiologie. [SOURCE: Page_90]

Mots-clés : blé, triticum durum, triticum aestivum, blé tendre, blé dur, rende- ment en grains, limitation de l’eau et de l’azote, espace phénotypique, compo- sants de rendement, nombre de grains. [SOURCE: Page_90]

Abstract [SOURCE: Page_90]

In the context of climate change and policies making, for a more sustainable agriculture using less inputs, grain yield is likely to be increasingly affected by [SOURCE: Page_90]

89 [SOURCE: Page_91]

abiotic constraints such as water and nitrogen (N) deficits. Depending on each specific environmental scenario, grain yield components may be differently affec- ted by these constraints because their underlying determinisms have their own genetic diversity. In addition, there are many tradeoffs between grain yield com- ponents, often related to the competition for resources, constraining the pheno- typic space of responses to abiotic stresses. In this study, we propose a method aiming at selecting sensitive and tolerant genotypes for grain yield covering the initial phenotypic space of the responses of grain yield components. We analyzed the sensitivity to water and N deficits, and the resulting phenotypic space of two diversity panels of 250 bread wheat and 200 durum wheat genotypes grown in seven and three field trials, respectively, with optimal conditions and conditions limited by N and/or water. We first studied how the responses of grain yield and its components varied under different environmental scenarios, observed that the response to stresses was strongly dependent on the interaction with experimental co-variables (site x year). We found that the phenotypic space of the sensitivity of grain yield and its components to water and N deficits was strongly constrained, which should be considered for the selection of sub-panels of diversity. Finally, by using random selection of the number of sites, we questioned the importance of the number of sites and their respective impact in the selection of subpanels. In addition to improving our knowledge of the relationships between the sen- sitivity of grain yield components to multiple constraints, this study is also of practical interest for the selection of subpanels covering the phenotypic space of grain yield sensitivity to abiotic constraints of the initial genetic panels, which can be used for ecophysiology studies. [SOURCE: Page_91]

Keywords : wheat, triticum durum, triticum aestivum, bread wheat, durum wheat, grain yield, water and nitrogen limitation, phenotipic space, yield com- poments, grain number. [SOURCE: Page_91]

90         Chapitre 4. Sensitivities of grain yield to water and nitrogen limitation [SOURCE: Page_92]

4.1     Introduction [SOURCE: Page_92]

Agriculture production must at the same time, (i) feed a rapidly growing glo- bal population, which exacerbates tensions over water use and increases the risk of food insecurity ; (ii). adapt to climate change, which increases the frequency and intensity of drought events (Lobell et al., 2011) ; and reduce it environmen- tal footprint (Pe’er et al., 2019), by using less fertilizers and water (Alamanos et al., 2019). However, water and nitrogen (N) can be considered as the main limi- ting environmental factors affecting grain yield (Jafarikouhini et al., 2021). In this context, maintaining grain yields will only be ensured by optimizing water and N use (Berger et al., 2016 ; Tardieu, 2012), though varietal improvement (Sinclair, 2017) and crop management practices. [SOURCE: Page_92]

Addressing this challenge is not straightforward. Indeed, climate change tends to increase the multiplicity of environmental scenarios, even within a given site, leading to increased interannual variations in agricultural production (Urban et al., 2015), mainly due to the multiplicity of drought scenarios (Fischers and Knutti, 2013). In addition, abiotic constraints tend to increase Genotype x Envi- ronment (G x E) interactions :). Thus, the best variety observed in one environ- ment may be a very poor choice in a different drought scenario. [SOURCE: Page_92]

Grain yield components may be differently affected by N and water deficits. Not only because the periods of sensitivity during the crop growth cycle of grain yield related physiological processes are not the same (Parent et al., 2017), but also because the underlying mechanisms have their own sensitivities and genetic de- terminisms. In each given scenario, a given "strategy" will therefore be beneficial, or not, to better maintain final grain yield, and grain quality (Tardieu, 2012). [SOURCE: Page_92]

In addition, there are many tradeoffs between grain yield components. As independent as mechanistically possible, grain yield components and their sensi- tivities can be correlated with each other in germplasms or diversity panels, due to the many tradeoffs, and constraints (Donovan et al., 2011) including evolution and selection, genetics, biophysics, and plants’ structure and architecture . Such [SOURCE: Page_92]

### 4.1. Introduction                                                                  91

constraints are strongly related to the competition for resources, the same pool of water, N and carbon serving to the different sink organs. As a results, a va- riety that tends to strongly reduce the number of spike per square meter under drought will tend to reduce less the number of grains per spike (Vahamidis et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_93]

To take these trade-offs into account, it is necessary to consider the different responses of grain yield components as a whole. The set of constraints and ge- netic correlations between traits defines the set of possibilities, called phenotypic space, representing only a part of the n-dimensional hypercube (set of biological variables considered). Among the best known concrete cases of highly constrai- ned phenotypic spaces, is the Leaf Economics Spectrum (Wright et al., 2004 ; Shi- pley et al., 2006), which defines trade-off between leaf resource allocation and acquisition. [SOURCE: Page_93]

For the breeder, it is therefore necessary to consider (i) the diversity of tem- poral scenarios of abiotic constraints ; (ii) the potential non-additive interactions between the tolerance to multiple-abiotic constraints ; and (iii) the diversity of responses of the underlying grain yield components. [SOURCE: Page_93]

In this study, we present a method to select sensitive and tolerant genotypes for grain yield, the number of spikes per square meter, the number of grains per square meter, and the average grain dry mass in response to LI conditions (water and N). This was performed in order to get contrasting sensitivities while maxi- mizing the diversity of the selected genotypes. The selected genotypes were then analyzed in the PHENOARCH platform (Chapter 5) and in the field (Chapter 6). . We analyzed the sensitivity to water and N deficits, and the resulting phenotypic space of two diversity panels of 250 bread wheat and 200 durum wheat geno- types grown in seven and three field trials, respectively, with optimal conditions and conditions limited by N and/or water. We found that the response to water and N stresses strongly depended on the interactions between experimental co- variables (site x year). We then described a method to select subsets of genotypes [SOURCE: Page_93]

92          Chapitre 4. Sensitivities of grain yield to water and nitrogen limitation [SOURCE: Page_94]

in genetic panels that cover phenotypic space. Finally, by using random selection of the number of sites, we questioned the importance of the number and diversity of environmental scenarios and their respective impact in the selection of subpa- nels. In addition to improving our knowledge of the relationships between the sensitivity of grain yield components to multiple constraints, this study is also of practical interest for the selection of subpanels covering the phenotypic space of grain yield sensitivity to abiotic constraints that can be used for ecophysiology analyses. [SOURCE: Page_94]

4.2     Material and methods [SOURCE: Page_94]

4.2.1    Genetic material [SOURCE: Page_94]

A pane of 235 bread wheat (Triticum aestivum L.) genotypes were selected from a diversity panel of 450 genotypes sampled from 4,506 accessions in a world- wide collection (Balfourier et al., 2019) by minimizing linkage disequilibrium and by imposing agronomic constraints on phenology and plant height (Paux et al., 2022). Among these 235 genotypes, 194 were grown in all field trials. [SOURCE: Page_94]

For durum wheat, a panel of 257 durum wheat genotypes was constitutied from 126 elite lines of worldwide origin registered between 1973 and 2015, 69 elite lines developed by the Center Research for Agricultural and Economics and the University of Foggia, Italy, and 61 lines randomly selected from 480 evolu- tionary pre-breeding lines derived from an open-pollinated population based on the undercrossing of about 650 accessions from wild subspecies and elite lines (David et al., 2014). For technical reasons, only 232 durum wheat genotypes were common to all trials. [SOURCE: Page_94]

4.2.2    Field trials and experimental conditions [SOURCE: Page_94]

Ten field trials conducted in the frame of the European project H2020 Solace and the French ANR project BreedWheat were analyzed in this study (seven sites [SOURCE: Page_94]

### 4.2. Material and methods                                                           93

over two years for T. aestivum, and three sites over one year for T. durum ; Table 4.1 and Fig. S7 ; note that an additional site with T. aestivum has not been analy- sed because of sever logging). In each trial, two treatments were tested, a control condition with optimal water and N supplies (named high-input, HI hereafter) and another condition with either low N input or rainfed conditions (named low- input, LI hereafter ; Table 4.2 and Fig. S8). Plots were 2.9 to 11.9 m-2 depending on the site. All crops were sown at the locally recommended date, with sowing density varying from 196 to 300 seeds m-2 for the bread wheat trials depending on the site, and of 350 seeds m-2 for durum wheat in all trial (Table 4.2). Each trial used a split-plot design in which the LI and HI treatments were randomized on main plots, cultivar was randomized on the sub-plots and each treatment was replicated two to three times. [SOURCE: Page_95]

### 4.2. Material and methods                                                        95

In all trials, soil water content at 30-cm and 60-cm below the soil surface was monitored throughout the growing season using tensiometric probes. All other crop inputs including weed, disease and pest control, and potassium, phosphate, and sulfur fertilizers, were applied at levels to prevent non-N nutrients or weeds, diseases and pests from limiting grain yield. [SOURCE: Page_97]

Among the seven bread wheat trials, the LN treatments consisted in low N supply under rainfed conditions in four trials (trials DEScap^2017, INRcle^2017, INRmon^2017, and SYNmoi^2017 ; Table 4.2), in rainfed conditions with high N supply similar to the HI treatment in one trial (BAYmil^2017), and rainfed conidi- tions and low N supply in two trials (ARVgre^2018 and SYNlev^2018 ; Table 4.2). In all durum wheat trials, the LI treatments were under rainfed conditions and low N supply compared to the corresponding HI treatment (Table 4.2). [SOURCE: Page_97]

In all trials, N fertilizer applications in the HI treatements were intended to replicate commercial practice. The growth stages and application rates were cal- culated based on provisional N balance associated with expected grain yields using the N sheet balance method described in Rémy and Hébert (1977). For the LI treatments with low N fertilizer rate, N fertilizer was reduced by 37% to 100% compared with the HI treatments. In the HI treatments with low water supply, irrigation was applied to ensure that water supply did not limit plant growth. In all trials, the LI treatments were rainfed [SOURCE: Page_97]

4.2.3   Crop measurements [SOURCE: Page_97]

Crop m Several plant variables were measured and analyzed jointly in this study. Crop emergence and heading dates were scored at least twice weekly du- ring two weeks around the scored growth stage. Growth stages were determined as the date when 50% of the plants in plot had reached the considered growth stage. In the bread wheat trials, the number of spikes was counted about one week after anthesis on two to four non-adjacent rows over 0.75 to 1 m (0.25 to 0.7 m2 ). At harvest maturity, all plots were harvested with a combiner. One to two [SOURCE: Page_97]

Grain number per square meter was calculated by dividing grain yield by the average grain dry mass. For all bread wheat trials, grain number per spike was calculated by dividing grain number per square me- ter by the spike number per square metter. Spike number per square meter, and thus grain number per spike, weere not determined in the durum wheat trials. All grain yields and average grain dry masses were expressed at 0% moisture. [SOURCE: Page_98]

The metrics rank genotype was used for genotype selection. In this study, we also applied specific conditions adapted to our dataset, which we do not consi- der as part of the method itself. We considered here only the genotypes that were present in at least two trials. In bread wheat, we have chosen only high-yielding genotypes with average grain yield > 5 t ha-1 in HI conditions, and within a flo- wering window of 13 days (average day-of-year of heading across all trials from 132 to 145). We selected the 20 genotypes with the lowest average rank of sensi- tivity and the 20 genotypes with the highest rank, considered respectively as the most “sensitive” and “tolerant” genotypes, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_100]

4.2.5    Data analysis [SOURCE: Page_100]

All data analyses were carried out using the R software (R Core Team, 2018). The correlation matrix of the heatmap, we used the function "heatmap" and "hclust" of the package R "stats" (correlation matrix with the method "euclidean" and the hierarchical clustering with the method of "ward.D". For the analysis of variances, we used the "anova" function of the "stats" package in R. For the correlations bet- ween the variables, we used a linear regression and for the correlation tables, we used the method of "pearson" with the function "rcorr" of the package R "Hmics" and the function "cor.test" of the package R "stats". [SOURCE: Page_100]

4.3     Results [SOURCE: Page_100]

by site/trials contrasts rather than targeted water and nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_100]

limitations [SOURCE: Page_100]

### 4.3. Results                                                                         99

on the considered trial, the observed range of grain yield differed, even under non-limiting conditions (HI), indicating an effect of the trial on grain yield in HI conditions. Under more limiting LI conditions, observed grain yield were glo- bally lower compared to that in HI but the difference depended on the trial. For example, the largest differences in grain yield between HI and LI was observed in the trial SYNlev^2018 and the lowest differences were observed in SYNmoi^2017 (Fig. 1). Note that because these trials were not repeated over several years, the respective effects of specific site and year cannot be separated and are therefore summarized into the effect of trial (defined here as Etrial ). By contrast we define the specific effect of the treatment within a trial as Etreatment . [SOURCE: Page_101]

In each trial, the correlation between LI and HI conditions was high and signi- ficant, even in trials in which the effect of the treatment was severe (SYNlev^2018, INRcle^2017 and DEScap^2017). It indicated that G x E interactions for grain yield were low compared to the effect of the treatment (Etreatmen ). While this was ex- pected in trials in which the effect of the LI conditions was low, this was observed even in trials for which the LI treatment was severe, and regardless of the origin of the stress, N and/or water. [SOURCE: Page_101]

To analyze the genetic correlations between trials and treatments, we perfor- med a hierarchical clustering of environments (trial x treatments), based on the ranking of each genotype for grain yield (Fig. 4.2). It appeared clearly that the environments clustered based on their the site x year, rather than the treatments. In other words, a genotype with high grain yield in one environment, was also one with the highest grain yield in the other environment of the same trial (low G x Etreatment compared to G x Etrial ). Overall, it indicated that one environment was more similar to the other environment of the same trial, even if they differed in terms of abiotic conditions (e.g. HI and LI conditions of the same trial) than to another environment, in another trial with similar abiotic conditions (e.g. HI conditions of two different trials). [SOURCE: Page_101]

Overall, it appeared that the effect of the trial Etrial , and so the effects of en- [SOURCE: Page_101]

### 4.3. Results                                                                               101

F IGURE 4.2 – Heat map of grain yield rank for 273 bread wheat genotype grown in the field in 14 treatment x trial combinations. The treatments x trial combinations and genotypes were group using a Hierarchical clustering analysis. [SOURCE: Page_103]

102         Chapitre 4. Sensitivities of grain yield to water and nitrogen limitation [SOURCE: Page_104]

vironment x management (other than water and N) provided stronger G x E in- teractions than those driven by the N and water treatments, and that, even in the trials in which the overall effect of the treatment was high. [SOURCE: Page_104]

Taken together, these results show that G x E interactions due to N and water treatments can be highly dependent on the trial, highlighting the need of several trials and a proper method, if one aim at selecting genotypes differing in their response to LI conditions (G x Etreatmen interactions). [SOURCE: Page_104]

4.3.2     Trials and treatments both participate to E and G x E effects on [SOURCE: Page_104]

grain yield components under nitrogen and drought constraints [SOURCE: Page_104]

The analysis of heading date for each trial and each treatment showed (Fig. 4.1, Table S11) (i) that earliness was genotype-dependent (± 20 days depending on the trial ; G effect), (ii) that heading dates were highly correlated between the two treatments of each trial (no G x E interaction), and this, on the 1 :1 line (no treatment-specific effect). These results indicated that treatment-specific effects on grain yield components could not be explained by any change of develop- ment rate between treatments (no G x E effect observed on heading date). On the other hand, the observed G effect (different heading dates between genotypes) may lead to differences in the timing of stresses between genotypes. This diffe- rence may differ between trials and may therefore result in different values of sensitivities calculated for the same genotype in different trials. Overall, in order to increase the robustness of the classification of genotypes according to their sen- sitivity to N or drought stresses, it is necessary to consider several trials that are different in their temporal scenarios of abiotic stresses. [SOURCE: Page_104]

Differences between trials were observed in the four grain yield components analyzed here, both for the median value of one component in one trial (Etrial ef- fect on one component) and for the range of values covered by the genotypes (G x Etrial ). In addition, for most grain yield components and most trials, the observed values in LI conditions were significantly correlated with those in HI conditions [SOURCE: Page_104]

### 4.3. Results                                                                      103

(Table S11, P < 0.01 in 24 out of the 26 tested correlations). This indicated, as for grain yield, that the G effect was high, compared to the G x Etreatment effect. [SOURCE: Page_105]

However, the correlation was not total for each component in each trial in- dicating some G x Etreatment effects. Overall, the data showed the importance of considering the trial effect and G x Etrial effect in order to properly analyze the diversity of responses to water and N deficits (G x Etreatment ). [SOURCE: Page_105]

4.3.3    Which component drives the diversity of the sensitivity of yield [SOURCE: Page_105]

to abiotic constraints [SOURCE: Page_105]

In order to understand how grain yield components could drive the sensiti- vity of grain yield to LI conditions, we first analyzed the relationships between the sensitivity of grain yield to LI conditions in each trial and that of its compo- nents, the number of grain per square meter and the individual grain dry mass (Fig. 4.3 and Table 4.3). The sensitivity of the number of grain per square meter was then itself analyzed as a function of its two underlying components, the sen- sitivity of the grain number per spike and the number of spike per meter square (Fig. 4.3 and Table 4.4). The sensitivity of grain number was highly correlated with that of grain yield in all trials (average r = 0.84, all P < 0.001), and overall largely more than that of individual grain dry mass (r ranged from -0.04 to 0.45, non-significant in two trials). The analysis of variance of the sensitivity of grain yield considering these two components as predictors resulted in similar conclu- sions, grain number explaining 10-times more variance than individual grain dry mass, and that, largely independently of the trial (Table 4.3). Overall, both ana- lyses indicate that the sensitivity of grain yield to LI condition (G x Etreatment on grain yield) was largely related to that of grain number per square meter rather than individual grain dry mass. [SOURCE: Page_105]

It was less clear which components of grain number (either grain number per spike or spike number per square meter) were most related to the sensitivity of grain yield to LI conditions (Fig. 4.3). For example, the sensitivity of the number [SOURCE: Page_105]

4.3. Results                                                                                         105 [SOURCE: Page_107]

of ears per square meter correlated with that of grain yield in the INRmon^2017 trial, while this was not the case for the sensitivity of the number of grains per ear. The opposite was observed in ARVgre^2018 and SYNmol^2017. (Fig. 4.3). [SOURCE: Page_107]

We then tested the relative contributions of the sensitivities of spike number per square meter and grain number per spike to that of grain number (and there- fore grain yield ; Fig. 4.3 and Table 4.4). Both underlying components explained the variance observed on the sensitivity of grain number (Table 4.4), with a higher effect for the number of grain per spike. This was also observed on the sensitivity of grain yield (Fig. 3), with most correlations (but not all, see above) between the sensitivity of grain yield and that of these two components being significant. [SOURCE: Page_107]

Overall, this analysis indicated that grain number per square meter drove most of the sensitivity of grain yield to LI conditions, but with different un- derlying components, either the number of spike per square meter (as in INR- mon^2017) or the grain number per spike (as in SYNev^2018). [SOURCE: Page_107]

4.3.4      A method for subseting genotypes and maximizing variability [SOURCE: Page_107]

in the sensitivity of grain yield and its components, and their [SOURCE: Page_107]

causal relationships [SOURCE: Page_107]

The results presented above demonstrate that (i) the effect of the trial Etrial could be very strong compared to that of the LI condition, (ii) that it was the same for the G x Etrial , indicating the need to consider different trials robustly classify genotypes based on their grain yield sensitivity to LI conditions, (iii) depending on the trial, the sensitivity of grain yield was explained by the diversity of the [SOURCE: Page_107]

Degree of   Sum of    Mean Factors                                                                   F-value   Pr(> F)   P-value freedom     squares   squares Spike number per square meter               1           8.79      8.79    4664      0         < 0.001 Grain number per spike                      1           21.40     21.40   11358     0         < 0.001 Environment : Spike number per square meter 5           1.06      0.21    122       0         < 0.001 Environment : Grain number per spike        5           0.31      0.06    32        0         < 0.001 Residuals                                   1114        2.10      0.00    NA        NA        NA height [SOURCE: Page_107]

106         Chapitre 4. Sensitivities of grain yield to water and nitrogen limitation [SOURCE: Page_108]

sensitivity of spike number per square meter or that of the number of grain per spike, indicating that an advantage on one of these components could have no effect on grain yield depending on the trial and therefore the genetic diversity of such traits could be lost when selecting genotype in such trials. [SOURCE: Page_108]

Taken together, these results indicated that a method considering several trials and the diversity of sensitivities to all grain yield components is necessary to se- lect genotypes representing the diversity of grain yield responses to LI conditions. Therefore, we proposed a method to select a subset of genotypes that maximizes diversity of grain yield sensitivity to water and N constraints (i) based on sensi- tivities calculated in different trials (ii) by giving more weight to trials in which constraints had stronger effects on grain yield, (iii) with a particular attention to the relationships between grain yield components and grain yield, i.e. covering the whole phenotypic space of grain yield sensitivities and its components. [SOURCE: Page_108]

The metrics used for genotype selection was the average rank of grain yield sensitivity calculated here for grain yield of each genotype, the ranks in each trial being weighted by an index depending on the average effect of the LI treatment of the considered trial (see the Materials and Method section). Genotypes with the lowest rank were therefore considered as the most “sensitive” and genotypes with the highest rank the most “tolerant”. We selected the 20 genotypes with the lowest and higher average rank of sensitivity and considered them as the most “sensitive” and “tolerant” genotypes, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_108]

In bread wheat, contributions of each trial to the average sensitivity of grain yield to Li conditions are indicated in the top-left corner of each panel of Fig.1. For example, the trial with the highest weight (higher contribution to the calcula- tion of average rank) was SYNlev^2018 followed by INRcle^2017 and DEScap^2017 which contributed 30.2, 22.1, 21.6 % to the rank of genotype sensitivity to LI conditions (Fig. 4.1). [SOURCE: Page_108]

We then checked if the distribution of the 40 genotypes (sub-panel) covered the phenotypic space of grain yield and its components in LI and HI conditions [SOURCE: Page_108]

Overall, we discriminated genotypes between those maintaining grain yield in LI condi- tions (“tolerant” genotypes) from those being more “sensitive” (Fig. 4.4D). This sensitivity was linked to the sensitivity of grain number in both the whole panel (see above) and our sub-panel (Fig. 4.4D). It was not the case for individual grain dry mass but the subset of genotypes covered the range of sensitivity observed in the whole panel (Fig. 4.4C). The sensitivity of grain number in our panel was due either to the sensitivity of the number of grains per spike (Fig. 4.4B) or the sensitivity of the number of spike per square meter (Fig. 4.4 A), as observed in the whole panel. [SOURCE: Page_109]

Overall, this selected sub-panel covered the phenotypic space of average sen- sitivities of grain yield components (Fig. 4.4), and we did not observe any kind of artefact which may have appeared in single trials (Fig. 4.1 and Fig. 4.3). The average ranks of sensitivity for all grain yield components of the selected bread wheat genotypes are given in Supplementary Information Table S$_9$. [SOURCE: Page_109]

In durum wheat, the results were generally similar to those for bread wheat As for bread wheat, we discriminated well “tolerant” and “sensitive genotypes” for grain yield. As in the whole panel, their grain yield sensitivity to LI conditions was related to their grain number sensitivity (Fig. 4.5B), and not to that of indi- vidual grain dry mass (Fig. 4.5A). The subset of genotypes covered the range of sensitivity observed in the whole panel for both underlying components of grain yield. As for grain yield we also check the effect of selection at the trial level (Fig. S9 and Fig. S10). ). The average ranks of sensitivity for all grain yield components of the selected durum wheat genotypes are given in Supplementary Information Table S10. [SOURCE: Page_109]

4.3.5     Robustness of genotype selection as a function of the number [SOURCE: Page_111]

of trials [SOURCE: Page_111]

The selection method for bread wheat genotypes, based on seven contrasting trials, respected the relationships between the sensitivities of grain yield compo- nents. Therefore, we investigated how the number of trials considered for selec- tion of sensitive and tolerant genotypes might affect the results by analysing the effect of the number of trials on selection similarity when all trials are considered (and considered here as the ground-truth). We performed two types of analysis (Fig. 4.6). For both analysis, the selection of genotypes was performed using the method described in the Material and Methods section. These analyses were not performed for durum wheat because of the low number trials. [SOURCE: Page_111]

The first analysis considers the relative effect of each trial on genotype selec- tion, starting with the trial with the highest stress-index and adding trials step by step, from the trial with the highest stress index to the trial with the lowest value. For example, we fond that trial SYNlev2018 had the strongest contribution (30%) to the final rank (Fig. 4.1). Selection of genotypes based on this trial only resulted in about 55% of similarity with the selection that considered all trials (Fig. 4.6). The addition of trials improved the similarity with the final selection, even for [SOURCE: Page_111]

### 4.4. Discussion                                                                 111

The second analysis considered random sampling (with replacement) of a number of trials between 1 and 7 (Fig. 4.6). This analysis showed that, on average, a selection based on a single trial resulted in about 20-25% similarity with our final selection. The number of genotypes selected was 40 out of 232 genotypes at the beginning, i.e. 17%, so this selection was close to a random selection of genotypes. The more trials were analyzed, the closer the selection was to our final selection. [SOURCE: Page_113]

Overall, both analyses showed that increasing the number of trials increased the similarity with the ground-truth selection, and that, even with trials in which the effect of the LI condition was low. [SOURCE: Page_113]

4.4     Discussion [SOURCE: Page_113]

4.4.1    Phenotypic structure of grain yield sensitivity to water and ni- [SOURCE: Page_113]

trogen limitations [SOURCE: Page_113]

In this study, we have clearly observed that grain yield sensitivity to water and N constraints was related to that of grain number per spike rather than indi- vidual grain dry mass. This was observed in all trials, in bread wheat and durum wheat. This result was expected in well-watered conditions, but not necessarily under water and N limiting conditions. We considered a large diversity of envi- ronmental scenarios, and we would have expected that final stresses in rainfed environments would have affected individual grain dry mass. Indeed, the sensi- tivity to water shortage is higher at around anthesis than at the vegetative and grain filling stages (Fischer, 1993 ; Reynolds et al., 2004). On the contrary, grain yield is more sensitive to N uptake during the vegetative stage than during the re- productive and maturation stages (Sharma et al., 1992), with an overlap between these two periods around the stage of stem elongation. In addition, the grain yield sensitivity to water stress would increase at higher N application rates. [SOURCE: Page_113]

112         Chapitre 4. Sensitivities of grain yield to water and nitrogen limitation [SOURCE: Page_114]

The correlation between grain yield and grain number has long been a matter of debate. Indeed, grain number and grain yield are correlated in many species (Sadras, 2021). The strong correlation between grain yield and grain number had been largely interpreted as a cause and effect relationship until Sinclair & Jamie- son challenged this view (Sinclair and Jamieson, 2008). Variation in grain number and grain yield would likely be caused by variations in resource accumulation, particularly N, by the crop (Sinclair and Jamieson, 2008). In our study, this re- lationship was valid under all tested conditions, in bread wheat, and in durum wheat, regardless of the environmental scenario, with a weak effect of individual grain dry mass, especially for bread wheat. Overall, these results rather validate the causal theory, according to which the number of grains (carbon sinks) would set a large part of the final grain yield very early in the cycle, with very little room for the sensitivity of individual grain dry mass in explaining the variance of final grain yield. [SOURCE: Page_114]

The sensitivity of the number of grains per square meter was linked to the sensitivity of the number of spike per square metter in some trials and sometimes to the number of grains per spike, depending on the trial. This is consistent with the fact that there are different sensitivity periods for the number of spike per square meter and the number of grains per spike (Parent et al., 2017). Indeed, the traits related to spike development (length of spike and number of spikelets) are rather sensitive during the early vegetative stages, i.e. the period of spike development (Vahamidis et al., 2014). In Parent el al. (2017), the number of grains per spikelet was very sensitive during a short period around flowering, possibly related to male or female sterility during gametocyte development (Saini et al., 1984 ; Saini and Aspinall, 1982), pollination (Ferris et al., 1998), or competition for carbon at an early stage of grain filling. The number of grains per spikelet is less sensitive to early environmental conditions, which could have affected spikelet development (Saini and Aspinall, 1982). In our study, the diversity of scenarios has therefore led to different sensitivities between components of grain number, and to make the best use of the genetic diversity of sensitivities of these two [SOURCE: Page_114]

### 4.4. Discussion                                                                   113

underlying grain number variables. [SOURCE: Page_115]

4.4.2   Subset genotypes cover the phenotypic space of the whole pa- [SOURCE: Page_115]

nel [SOURCE: Page_115]

In our study, the set of correlations between grain yield, its components, and their sensitivities, defined a constrained phenotypic space. Our genotype selec- tion respected this phenotypic space, in the sense that it kept all its components, the relationships between variables and the genetic variability leading to these relationships. [SOURCE: Page_115]

Several types of constraints can lead to strong correlations between a priori independent biological variables, leading to a bounded phenotypic space : evo- lution and selection, genetics, biophysics, and plant structure (Donovan et al., 2011). In particular, here, varieties/species have been able to evolve in the same direction for several traits because they were subjected to the same environmental or agronomic constraints (Mayr and Provine, 1998). Evolution and selection have therefore been able to constrain this space, in particular selection for grain qua- lity, by selecting varieties with fewer grains under limiting conditions but main- taining quality grains. As these relationships are similar in durum wheat (tetra- ploid) and bread wheat (hexaploid), the hypothesis of strong genetic constraints (proximity, link between genomic regions) is less likely than the hypothesis of common evolutionary and agronomic constraints. [SOURCE: Page_115]

4.4.3   Possible improvements of the genotype selection method [SOURCE: Page_115]

In this study we described a method to sample genotpyes in durum and bread wheat genetic panels the respectd the phenotypic space of the whole panels. Ho- wever, these relationships were only verified afterwards, the method being based on grain yield only. Although a given number of safeguards have been determi- ned (contrasted trials, weightings, validation afterwards), it could be interesting to build a multi-criteria selection on the rank of the sensitivity of the grain yield, [SOURCE: Page_115]

114         Chapitre 4. Sensitivities of grain yield to water and nitrogen limitation [SOURCE: Page_116]

as well as on those of its components, to guarantee, from the start, that the pheno- typic space is fully covered in the final selection. Currently we have not finalized and/or use such method, mainly because of the lack of time (the following ac- tivities for my thesis and in SolAce depended on it), and because of a needed consensus between different actors in the SolACE project. [SOURCE: Page_116]

4.4.4     Conclusions [SOURCE: Page_116]

The set of correlations between yield, its components and their sensitivities defined a very constrained phenotypic space. Our genotypic selection respec- ted this phenotypic space, in the sense that it preserved all its components, the relationships between variables and the genetic variability leading to these rela- tionships. Several types of constraints can lead to strong correlations between independent biological variables "a priori", leading to a bounded phenotypic space : evolution and selection, genetics, biophysics, plant structure. In particular, here, varieties/species were able to evolve in the same direction for several traits, because they were subject to the same environmental or agronomic constraints (Mayr and Provine, 1998). Evolution and breeding were thus able to constrain this space, particularly breeding for grain quality, by selecting varieties with fe- wer grains under limiting conditions, but maintaining grain quality. As these re- lationships are similar in durum wheat (tetraploid) and bread wheat (hexaploid), the hypothesis of strong genetic constraints (proximity, linkage between genomic regions) is less likely than the hypothesis of common evolutionary and agrono- mic constraints. [SOURCE: Page_116]

The genotypes selected in this study, where used in several phenotyping ex- periments under semi-field conditions at Clermont-Ferrand, France (for bread wheat), and Groux-les-Bains (for durum wheat), and in platforms to phenotype the response of shoot growth (in the PhenoArch plateform at UMR LEPSE, see chapter 6 of this thesis), and root growth and architecture (in the RooPhAir plat- form at UCL, Louvain, Belgium, and 4PMI, UMR Agrecology, Dijon) to water and N limiations. The results of the PhenoArch experiment are presented in the follo- [SOURCE: Page_116]

## CHAPITRE 5

Tillering structures the genetic variability of wheat [SOURCE: Page_121]

vegetative growth and its plasticity under water deficit [SOURCE: Page_121]

Abstract [SOURCE: Page_121]

Leaf growth in response to water stress in wheat plants is determined by seve- ral factors, such as tillering and leaf number as well as their time-dependent rates. In this chapter, we present the trade-offs between the components of leaf expan- sion at the whole plant level and their sensitivities to water deficit. We explain that the observed diversity of leaf growth under different water scenarios is a re- sult of the diversity of tillering, at least more than that of the rate of leaf emission or elongation of individual leaves. The plasticity of leaf area was mainly determi- ned by that of tillering under our growing conditions. In addition, we show the dynamics of leaf growth, as well as the respective shares of its subcomponents, their plasticities and their mutual relationships. We used simple formalisms for the response of the leaf area components, as well as the associated genetic para- meter structure that can be used for modelling. As an output, we identify tillering [SOURCE: Page_121]

119 [SOURCE: Page_121]

120                          Chapitre 5. Tillering structures the genetic variability [SOURCE: Page_122]

as a trait that drives leaf area underwater deficit, and this is the subcomponent that explains the genetic variability of leaf area in wheat. [SOURCE: Page_122]

Key words : bread wheat, drought sensitivity, durum wheat, genetic variabi- lity, leaf expansion, phenotypic plasticity, tillering. [SOURCE: Page_122]

5.1      Introduction [SOURCE: Page_122]

Agriculture is facing both the increasing global demand for food and cli- mate change. Increasing frequency of drought events, and other extreme weather events (Lobell et al., 2011), increases interannual variability in crop yields in dif- ferent regions of the globe (Urban et al., 2015), mainly due to multiple drought scenario (Fischer and Knutti, 2013). [SOURCE: Page_122]

In this context, plants constantly adapt to changes in their microclimate and soil water status, particularly through the control of transpiration (Faralli et al., 2019a, 2019b ; Hatfield and Dold, 2019). Transpiration is controlled in the short term by stomatal conductance and in the long term by leaf area dynamics (Sin- clair, 2017). Control of stomatal conductance and leaf expansion are thus key players in the genetic and environmental variability of transpiration (Tardieu and Parent, 2017). However, climate change appears to increase the frequency of com- bined high temperature and drought events (Barnabás et al., 2008 ; Lobell et al., 2011 ; Webber et al., 2018). In this case, the decrease in stomatal conductance can lead to over-heating (Rizhsky et al., 2002), with significant and sometimes irre- versible consequences. [SOURCE: Page_122]

Reduction of leaf growth under water deficit allows to adjust transpiration without negative heating. The use of traits associated with leaf expansion as ge- netic levers in plant breeding may seem sensible but is not straightforward. In- deed, the control of leaf area and its dual consequences on photosynthesis and transpiration is a major player in Genotype x Environment (G x E) interactions. For example, under adverse conditions, every millimeter of water saved during [SOURCE: Page_122]


---
