### 5.1. Introduction                                                                   121

the vegetative phase allows for a more favorable water status during the later stages of flowering and grain filling (Sinclair, 2017). In contrast, under moderate drought, the reduction in biomass accumulation accompanying lower leaf area and transpiration is not compensated for during the growth cycle, resulting in lower biomass and grain production (Tardieu, 2012). In addition, while in the short-term expansive growth can be view as largely uncoupled from structural growth (Hilty et al., 2021), in the longer term feedbacks can occur between the “container” and the “content” (e.g. carbon sink/source relationships), so that a greater leaf area can lead to a greater final biomass. Thus, the best variety obser- ved in one environment may be a very poor choice in a different drought scenario (Tardieu, 2012). [SOURCE: Page_123]

"Tuning growth to the environmental demand" (Rymen and Sugimoto, 2012) requires focusing not only on the plants constitutive growth potential but also on its ability to modulate its growth under drought (plasticity, Bradshaw, 1965). Plasticity of plant architectural traits allows plants to modify their architecture when environment changes (Pierik et al., 2021), increasing the range of adapta- tion of a species (Bacher et al., 2022). It is therefore at the basis of plant perfor- mance in both natural and crop production environments (Pierik et al., 2021). Phenotypic plasticity occurs at all levels of organization within plants (Funk et al., 2007). This is particularly true for expansive growth (Belaygue et al., 1996). Indeed, the whole-plant leaf area is the consequence of three underlying pro- cesses (branching or tillering, individual-leaf expansion rate, and leaf apperance rate). The fact that these processes have their own timing within the crop-cycle and potentially their own genetic variability and response to water deficit makes it complex to analyze the genetic variability of whole plant growth under fluctua- ting environments. There is no single response to water deficit, but potentially as many as there are water deficit scenarios (duration, intensity, timing for example) (Koch et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_123]

There is potentially a large variability of stress-adaptive plasticity in wheat, as found in wheat relatives (Leveau et al., 2021 ; Suneja et al., 2019).The rate of leaf [SOURCE: Page_123]

122                           Chapitre 5. Tillering structures the genetic variability [SOURCE: Page_124]

appearance is often considered to be stable and independent of the environment (except for photoperiod and temperature). In the only paper to our knowledge that considered the relative sensitivities of the underlying components of shoot expansion (in white clover), Belaygue et al. (1996) found that leaf appearance rate was the least sensitive process. However, in both durum and bread wheat, wa- ter deficit has been shown to decrease the rate of leaf appearance. In addition, Baumont et al. (2019) showed that the rate of leaf appearance in wheat plants is sensitive to the carbon status of the plant, and thus to the environmental condi- tions driving this status (intercepted radiation, photoperiod, CO$_2$ , ...), and that genetic variability exists for this trait. Overall, the relative contribution of leaf ap- pearance rate to the genetic variability of whole-plant expansive growth and its plasticity under drought remains largely unknown. [SOURCE: Page_124]

In most studied species, there is a large variability in the response of indivi- dual leaf expansion to soil water potential (SWP ; e.g. Parent et al., 2009 in rice ; Welcker et al., 2011 in maize), as is its response to other environmental conditions in wheat (VPD ; Leveau et al., 2021). In non-tillering maize hybrids, the response of individual leaves to SWP correlated to that of whole plant (Lacube et al., 2020). However, its relative influence in tillering species such as wheat species has been far less studied. [SOURCE: Page_124]

Tillering traits under drought have long been a breeding target, especially for drought-prone environment. However, most studies have focused on the in- trinsic value of tillering potential, with the aim of reducing tillering. Indeed, the advantage of reduced-tillering traits is close to a “drought avoidance stra- tegy”, reducing leaf area in order to shift soil water availability from pre- to post-anthesis when periods of drought stress are frequent (Berry et al., 2003). A reduced-tillering tin (tiller inhibition) gene (Atsmon and Jacobs, 1977) increases grain yield in dryland environments but not under wetter conditions (Hendriks et al., 2016 ; Reynolds et al., 2009). While it appears that tillering is a key process for phenotypic plasticity (Sanad et al., 2016), we could find only few results on the plasticity of free-tillering genotypes and the associated genetic diversity, scatters [SOURCE: Page_124]

### 5.1. Introduction                                                                 123

into more general studies. [SOURCE: Page_125]

A purely experimental approach cannot explore the consequences on yield of each combination of plasticity-related traits across the full range of drought scenarios. Model-based ideotyping (Martre et al., 2015 ; Parent and Tardieu, 2014 ; Rötter et al., 2015) uses a crop model with genetic parameters, capable of simu- lating the Genotype x Environment x Management interactions under a wide va- riety of present and future local scenarios (Parent et al., 2018), combined with phenotypic measurements of the genetic variability of its parameters. Such a mo- del therefore needs to be built according to the possibilities of large-scale phe- notyping (genetic diversity for hundreds of genotypes) (Lacube et al., 2017), and defining the constraints between parameters. It allows the model to not be over- parameterized and to define idéotypes that are achievable by breeding. The set of constraints and genetic correlations between traits defines the set of possibilities, called phenotypic space (Shipley et al., 2006 ; Wright et al., 2004), representing only a part of the hyper-cube of parameters (Raup and Michelson, 1965). [SOURCE: Page_125]

In the case of whole plant shoot expansion, if its underlying components and their sensitivities to environmental constraints were fully independent, with dif- ferent genetic controls, this would imply many genetic parameters that could be very difficult to measure in hundreds of genotypes. In one sense, these com- ponents could all have their own response to environmental constraints, at dif- ferent time scales, and could integrate differently the “history” of the plant micro- environment. However, the independency of these traits can be questioned be- cause (i) some common underlying processes can drive several components, such as water potential and turgor, and (ii) the potential of one process (e.g., Individual leaf expansion rate) under well-watered conditions is linked to its sensitivity to water deficit, the biggest plants being the most sensitive to water stress (e.g. Wel- cker et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_125]

In this study, we used a non-destructive imaging platform in order to analyze the temporal shoot growth dynamics under well-defined water deficit scenarios [SOURCE: Page_125]

124                         Chapitre 5. Tillering structures the genetic variability [SOURCE: Page_126]

(Jin et al., 2012) and determine the links and trade-off between the components of shoot expansion and their sensitivities to water deficit. We conducted a large experiment in a high throughput platform ( PHENOARCH ; Cabrera-Bosquet et al., 2016 ; Prado et al., 2018) on 84 bread wheat (Triticum turgidum L. subsp. durum (Desf.) Husn.) and durum wheat (T. aestivum) genotypes in four drought scena- rios. We combined automatic measurements of shoot area over time (> 25,000 measurements) with manual measurements of individual leaf length, tiller num- ber and leaf number over time (> 10,000 measurements for each trait), to analyze the time courses of shoot area expansion and its underlying components under drought scenarios. We calculated the rates of each process and their sensitivity to SWP for each genotypes (Table 5.1). Our results demonstrate that the observed di- versity of plasticity for shoot area expansion is mostly driven by that of tillering. In addition, the most plastic component under drought was tillering, explaining more than two third of the phenotypic plasticity observed at whole-plant scale. This work can have large consequences in (i) ecophysiology, by describing the response of specific processes in a large genetic diversity of bread wheat and du- rum wheat, (ii) in breeding, by determining the genetic variability for target traits under drought in these two species, and (iii) in modelling, by simplifying the pa- rameterization of the plasticity of leaf area under drought and by describing the phenotypic space of the shoot expansion traits that can be used in ideotyping studies. [SOURCE: Page_126]

126                          Chapitre 5. Tillering structures the genetic variability [SOURCE: Page_128]

5.2      Results [SOURCE: Page_128]

5.2.1     Dynamic responses of leaf area expansion and underlying pro- [SOURCE: Page_128]

cesses to water deficit [SOURCE: Page_128]

The dynamics of whole-plant leaf area (shoot area, SA) expansion was ana- lyzed for 49 genotypes of bread wheat and 37 genotypes of durum wheat un- der a well-watered scenario (S$_1$) and three water-deficit scenarios (S$_2$ to S$_4$) in the high-throughput plant phenotyping platform PhenoArch (Cabrera-Bosquet et al., 2016). The time course of SA versus thermal time after leaf-3 emergence were fitted to a cubic smoothing spline function (> 36 time points per plant, see Material and Methods for details ; Fig. 5.1A). For all plants under well-watered conditions (scenario S$_1$), time courses of SA showed a typical sigmoid curve with a period of exponential increase, followed by an almost-linear increase, a period of slowing-down, a maximum value, and a decrease due to leaf senescence after anthesis (Fig. 5.1A). [SOURCE: Page_128]

Whole-plant leaf expansion rate (shoot expansion rate, SER), calculated as the first derivation of SA curves had a bell shape (Fig. 5.1B), with maximum values between 400 and 800°Cd after leaf-3 appearance depending on the genotype. The three water deficit scenarios affected the time course of SER. In scenario S4 (a 31-d period of water deficit starting at the beginning of tillering), as illustrated in Figure 1B for the durum wheat cultivar Balsamo, SER increased similarly to that of well-watered plants during a first period, and then increased more slowly from around 250°Cd after leaf-3 appearance, when SWP decreased below -0.2 MPa (Fig. 5.1D). It resulted that the relative value of SER of droughted plants compared to that of well-watered plants was close to one until around 20°Cd after L-3 appearance (Fig. 5.1C), when the soil water potential (SWP) was about 0.2 MPa (Fig. 5.1D), and then decreased almost linear as SWP continued to decrease. When plotted against SWP, during a period combining maximum values of SER in well-watered conditions (S1) and between the time at which SWP decreased below -0.2 MPa and the day of first rewatering, the relative SER showed a linear [SOURCE: Page_128]

F IGURE 5.1 – Example of calculation of dynamic variables and their sensitivity to soil water potential. Data are for whole-plant leaf area (shoot area, SA) for the durum wheat cultivar Balsamo in the well-watered (control, scenario 1) and the water deficit scenario S4 (long water deficit). (A) Time courses of SA versus thermal time after leaf-3 appearance. A cubic smoothing spline (lines) was fitted to experimental data (circles). For the control treatment, the spline function was fitted to the data of all the plants (n = 3 to 6 replicates depending on the group), while for the water deficit scenario, the data for each plant were fitted separately. (B) Whole-plant leaf area expansion rate (shoot expansion rate, SER) versus thermal time after leaf-3 appearance. SER was calculated as the first derivative of the spline function equation fitted to SA versus thermal time after leaf- 3 appearance. (C) Relative SER versus thermal time after leaf-3 appearance. Relative SER was calculated by dividing SER of each plant in the water-deficit scenario (red curve in B) by that of the plants in the well-watered scenario (blue curve in B). (D) Soil water potential (SWP) versus thermal time after leaf-3 appearance for the well-watered (blue circles and curves) and water- deficit (red symbols and lines). As in (A), for the control treatment, data of all the plants (n = 3 to 6 replicates) were fitted together, while for the water deficit scenario data, the data of each plant were fitted separately. In (B), (C), and (D), the vertical dashed lines indicate the period beginning when SER in the control treatment was at 30% of its maximum value and ending when it had decreased by 20% of its maximum value. In (D) the gray band indicates the period beginning when SWP in water deficit scenario reached to -0.2 MPa and ending when at the plants were first rewatered. (E) relative SER versus SWP during the consensus period defined by the vertical dashed lines and the gray band. Solid line is linear regression forced to 1 when SWP is null. The slope of this regression corresponds to the sensitivity of SER to SWP. [SOURCE: Page_129]

128                          Chapitre 5. Tillering structures the genetic variability [SOURCE: Page_130]

response to soil water deficit (Fig. 5.1E). All genotypes showed similar SER time course and response to SWP, but with different absolute values (Fig. 5.2). [SOURCE: Page_130]

Other drought scenarios affected SER in a similar way but with different ti- mings (Fig. 5.2). For example, in scenarios S$_3$, for which plant watering was redu- ced 10 d later compared to S$_2$ and S$_4$, for most genotypes SER started to diverge from that of well-watered plants slightly latter compared to S$_2$ and S$_4$ plants (Fig. 5.2C). SER of early-rewatered plants (S$_2$) diverged from that of non-rewatered plants (S$_4$) rapidly after rewatering but with values lower than those of well- watered plants for 450 to 550°Cd after rewatering depending on genotype, and exceeded those of well-watered plants after anthesis (Fig. 5.2C). The time per- iod defined above to calculate the sensitivity of SER to SWP resulted, for all the genotypes, in a unique response in the three tested water-deficit scenarios (Fig. 5.2D). [SOURCE: Page_130]

Similar analyses of the time course of absolute values, rates, relative rates and sensitivities to SWP were performed for the three underlying components of shoot area (i.e. number of appeared tillers, number of appeared leaves, and leaf length ; Table 5.1). The tiller appearance rate (TiR) of well-watered plants (S$_1$), calculated from the number of appeared tillers over time, had a time course very- similar to that of SER (Fig. 5.3 and Supplementary Information Fig. S15), but with maximum values occurring slightly sooner compared to SER. TiR of droughted plants diverged from that well-watered plants at around -0.2 MPa, similarly to SER. This resulted in a unique linear response of relative TiR and SWP in the three water-deficit scenarios for each genotype (Fig. 5.3 and Supplementary In- formation Fig. S15) but with lower sensitivity than for SER. [SOURCE: Page_130]

Because water-deficit can affect the growth duration of individual organs more than their initiation and appearance rate (Verbraeken et al., 2021), we ana- lyzed the rate of elongation of all growing leaves on the main stem together, to avoid the a compensating effect by duration. Cumulated main-stem leaf length over time for droughted plants was closer to that of well-watered plants than [SOURCE: Page_130]

130                          Chapitre 5. Tillering structures the genetic variability [SOURCE: Page_132]

was observed for SA and the number of appeared tillers (Fig. 5.3). This resulted in small differences in the elongation rate of individual main-stem leaves (LER) of plants in the four scenarios (Fig. 5.3 and Supplementary Information Fig. S16), and a lower sensitivity of LER to SWP than that of TiR and SER. [SOURCE: Page_132]

In all scenarios and for all genotypes, leaf appearance rate (LAR) on the main stem calculated between leaf-3 and flag-leaf appearance showed a slight decrease with time (Fig. 5.3 and Supplementary Information Fig. S17). It resulted that the sensitivity of LAR to SWP was very small, and even positive in some genotypes (Supplmentary Tables S$_3$ and S$_4$). [SOURCE: Page_132]

The dynamic responses of leaf area expansion and underlying processes to water deficit were analyzed for 86 genotypes, allowing analysis of the relative impact of genetic diversity and phenotypic plasticity of the three processes un- derlying the variability of whole plant leaf expansion under drought. [SOURCE: Page_132]

5.2.2     Under well-watered conditions, the genetic variability of tiller [SOURCE: Page_132]

appearance rate is the main contributor to that of whole plant [SOURCE: Page_132]

leaf expansion [SOURCE: Page_132]

To understand the relative contribution of the genetic variability of the three underlying processes to the genetic variability of shoot expansion in well-watered conditions, we first compared the coefficients of variation of the maximum rates for shoot expansion (SERmax ), tiller appearance (TiRmax ), leaf appearance (LARmean ) and individual main-stem leaf elongation (LERmax ) in well-watered conditions (Fig. 5.4). For both species, the genetic variability of LARmean and LERmax was small compared to that of SERmax , indicated by lower coefficients of variation. On the contrary, that of TIRmax was higher than that of SERmax , especially in bread wheat. [SOURCE: Page_132]

The first dimension of a principal component analysis (Supplementary Infor- mation Fig. S18) indicated covariations of TiRmax and SERmax , while the two other [SOURCE: Page_132]

### 5.2. Results                                                                              133

components LERmax and LARmax were associated with SERmax on the second di- mension, explaining less variance. These results were confirmed by an analysis of variance (Table 5.2), which showed that the three components all explaining parts of the variance of SERmax , but with a higher total variation that can be attributed to TIRmax . Looking at the bivariate relationships between such variables showed significant relationships between SERmax and TiRmax (Fig. 5.5), either in each spe- cies or considering the two species together. This was not the case for LARmax and LERmax for which the correlation with SERmax was not significant for at least one the two species. As suggested by the principal component analysis, TiRmax was largely correlated with the final leaf number (FLN), which in turn was also associated with SERmax . [SOURCE: Page_135]

Overall, tillering was the underlying process with the highest relative varia- bility, and the most associated with shoot expansion, indicating a higher relative contribution to the genetic variability of shoot expansion, compared with leaf ap- pearance and individual-leaf elongation. [SOURCE: Page_135]

5.2.3    The plasticity of whole plant leaf area expansion under drought [SOURCE: Page_135]

is driven by that of tiller appearance rate [SOURCE: Page_135]

A similar analysis was performed in order to test the relative contributions of the sensitivity to soil water deficit of the three underlying processes to that of whole plant leaf area expansion. In both species, the sensitivity of TiR to soil wa- ter deficit had range similar to that of shoot expansion (both SER and TiR were [SOURCE: Page_135]

### 5.2. Results                                                                                    135

null at around -1 MPa ; Fig. 5.6). On the contrary, the sensitivity of LAR and LER was close to 0 MPa-1 in both species (Fig. 5.6) and that of LAR was even negative for most genotypes (Supplementary Information Table S12 and S13). The princi- pal component analysis performed on all variables (Supplementary Information Fig. S18) suggested that STiR was associated with SSER (on dimension 1), while less variance was explained by SLAR and SLER (on dimensions 2 and 3). This was confirmed by an analysis of variance between SSER and the three underlying sen- sitivities (Table 5.3). While the three underlying sensitivities were significantly associated with SSER , less variance was explained by SLAR and SLER compared to STiR . In both species, SSER and STIR were significantly correlated (Fig. 5.7). It was not the case for SLAR and SLER , which were not significantly correlated with SSER for at least one species and when both species were considered together. As previously found in other species (parent et al., 2009 in rice ; Welcker et al., 2011 in maize), the SSER was negatively correlated with SERmax . To the contrary, it was not expected but for both species SLER and STiR were significantly correlated, in- dicating similar sensitivities of the rates of leaf elongation and tiller appearance. [SOURCE: Page_137]

Overall, the above analyses showed that the sensitivity of whole plant leaf area expansion was the emergent property of that of the three underlying pro- cesses, with the STiR being the main contributor, followed by SLER and SLAR . [SOURCE: Page_137]

138                           Chapitre 5. Tillering structures the genetic variability [SOURCE: Page_140]

5.3      Discussion [SOURCE: Page_140]

5.3.1     Non-destructive recording of the temporal dynamics of shoot [SOURCE: Page_140]

growth processes under drought [SOURCE: Page_140]

In this study, we used a high-throughput plant phenotyping platform to ana- lyze the genetic variability of growth processes and their responses to soil water deficit. We followed the temporal dynamics of whole leaf area expansion, indivi- dual main-sem leaf appearance and elongation, and tiller appearance to associate their relative rates to the water deficit experienced at the same time, whether it is different according to the plant and/or the genotype (Jin et al., 2012). However, these results were obtained in pots, under controlled conditions, and their trans- posability to the field can be questioned. First, the ranking of genotypes made on the sensitivity of the number of spikes per square meter in the field was also partly found on TiR and/or the growth of the whole plant (Supplementary Infor- mation Fig. S12). The two genotypes of bread wheat of group A chosen to be the least sensitive in the field were also the least sensitive in the platform. The same was true for durum wheat. This conclusion is supported by previous studied sho- wing that traits related to growth potential and growth sensitivity to water deficit are robust and conserved in the field and in the platform (Tardieu et al., 2015). Al- though in the platform the number of tillers per plants was much higher than in the field, the relative responses of shoot growth processes to soil water deficit in the platform can be extrapolated to the field, or at least the classification of geno- types found here can be considered as similar in the field and under controlled conditions. [SOURCE: Page_140]

In this study, a very large experimental effort was dedicated to the non-destructive measurements of leaf dimensions and tiller numbers over time (> 10’000 data points each). Indeed, up to know, while there have been some attempts in the li- terature, there is no automatic procedure to automatically measure in an imaging platforms trait such as tiller numbers or individual-leaf length for large plants. As we demonstrated here that STiR was a crucial trait to explain the whole-plant [SOURCE: Page_140]

### 5.3. Discussion                                                                   139

sensitivity, new procedure, probably based on deep learning methods should be rapidly developed for analyzing new germplasms or for genetic analyses of such traits. [SOURCE: Page_141]

5.3.2     Structure of genetic variability of whole-plant leaf area expan- [SOURCE: Page_141]

sion under well-watered conditions [SOURCE: Page_141]

To better understand the structure of the genetic variability of shoot expan- sion we analyzed the respective impacts of the underlying processes and their co- variations. Indeed, the set of constraints and genetic correlations between traits defines the set of possibilities, informing both plant physiologists and breeder about the feasible combinations of traits. In the case of leaf expansion, for example, this question is "can we find a genotype that produce more tillers, more leaves, and larger leaves or is there constraints (e.g., developmental or carbon source- sink ratio) which result in a trade-off between the components of whole-plant growth. [SOURCE: Page_141]

Our working hypothesis, that the genetic variabilities of tiller appearance, leaf appearance and individual-leaf elongation rates could drive all together that of shoot expansion rate was confirmed, as previously found in white clover (Be- laygue et al., 1996, considering branching rather than tillering). Each of these three components was associated with the genetic diversity of shoot expansion. The respective impact of these components followed their genetic relative varia- bility (here quantified by their coefficient of variation), with a higher proportion of the genetic diversity of shoot expansion explained by that of TiR, followed by LER and LAR. [SOURCE: Page_141]

Two other processes participated to structure this genetic variability, plant development and early vigor. As found in maize (Parent et al., 2018), final leaf number was the main driver of cycle duration (compared to leaf appearance rate, not shown), and was therefore associated with maximum value of growth pro- cesses (SERmax , TiRmax , LERmax) because these processes were integrated over a [SOURCE: Page_141]

140                          Chapitre 5. Tillering structures the genetic variability [SOURCE: Page_142]

longer time. This effect was also visible on maximum rates. In particular, TiRmax was positively associated with FLN, as predicted by the Fibonacci series that links leaf and tiller appearance (Bos and Neuteboom, 1998). [SOURCE: Page_142]

The surface area of leaf 2 on the main-stem, a proxy of plant early vigor (Re- betzke and Richards, 1999) presented counter-intuitive correlations, by being ne- gatively associated with SERmax and TIRmax , probably due to the negative cor- relation between the size of earlier organs and cycle duration (as observed for maize leaves, Lacube et al., 2020). [SOURCE: Page_142]

To summarize, the genetic variability of shoot expansion was highly structu- red in both species, with a main contribution of cycle duration via TiR and overall, the relative contributions of each of the three underlying processes. [SOURCE: Page_142]

5.3.3     Plasticity of whole-plant leaf area expansion [SOURCE: Page_142]

We questioned the plasticity of whole-plant leaf area expansion at two levels. The first question was to which sub-variables is the plasticity of leaf growth under water deficit due ? The second was whether this calls into question the structure of leaf expansion shown above under well-irrigated conditions. In other words, how water deficit affects the relationships between traits. [SOURCE: Page_142]

We first showed that the sensitivity of leaf growth at the whole-plant level was a consequence of the sensitivities of its three components (all significant in the anova). However, most of the variance in SSER was explained by STiR . Thus, water deficit did not alter the structure of the genetic variability of shoot expan- sion, with tiller appearance being the main explanatory process of this genetic di- versity. However, under water deficit, the phenotypic space appeared even more constrained. Indeed, a strong correlation appeared between STIR and SLER , indica- ting that a genotype that strongly decreases its number of appeared tillers under water deficit, also strongly decreases its individual leaf growth. [SOURCE: Page_142]

In the same way, an important issue for breeding in near-optimal conditions [SOURCE: Page_142]

### 5.3. Discussion                                                                     141

is to develop a genotype with a high potential of growth and a low-sensitivity to drought while these two traits appear as negatively correlated in many crops (e.g., Welcker et al., 2011 in maize ; Parent et al., 2009 in rice). In this study, this constraint was also found in both durum and bread wheat. [SOURCE: Page_143]

5.3.4    Genetic variability of tillering for future drought tolerant wheat [SOURCE: Page_143]

cultivars [SOURCE: Page_143]

In environments subject to severe drought at the end of the growth cycle, a breeding strategy may be to favor varieties with limited leaf growth, to maintain a satisfactory soil water status at the end of the cycle, during grain filling (Sin- clair, 2017). Our study here shows that focusing on tillering control can be a good strategy. First, we showed that TiR has proportionally larger genetic diversity than LAR or LER. Moreover, excess tillering is a carbon sink, of which at least the structural part will not participate in yield in case of premature senescence (Kebrom et al., 2012). A selection strategy based on this high genetic variability and/or genes (e.g., TIN gene ; Atsmon and Jacobs, 1977 ; Hendriks et al., 2016) or identified QTLs (Wang et al., 2016) therefore seems adequate for environments where the probability of having sufficient rainfall at the end of the cycle is very low, thus not requiring any particular tillering plasticity, but instead a low consti- tutive number of tillers. [SOURCE: Page_143]

However, this type of environment is not the majority and there are gene- rally as many drought scenarios as site x year, so a favorable selection strategy would be to focus on adaptive processes and their plasticity (Rymen and Sugi- moto, 2012 ; Tardieu, 2012). Here, we have shown that tillering was the most plas- tic growth process compared to other growth components, and largely respon- sible for whole-plant growth plasticity under water deficit. Although somewhat less plastic, leaf elongation also appears to be a good target for modifying whole- plant leaf area plasticity. [SOURCE: Page_143]

142                          Chapitre 5. Tillering structures the genetic variability [SOURCE: Page_144]

5.3.5     Consequences for crop modelling and ideotype studies [SOURCE: Page_144]

This study can have large impact for process-based modelling and simula- tions of the genetic variability. Indeed, we observed a highly structured gene- tic diversity of the components of shoot expansion and their sensitivities to soil water deficit. This can either greatly simplify the formalisms of shoot expansion in process-based model or indicate the crucial constraints between parameters which need to be taken into account in ideotype studies. For example, the sensiti- vity of individual leaf expansion was correlated with that of tillering. This results that only one genotypic parameter should be considered in ideotype studies or for analyzing the impact of the genetic diversity at large scale (Parent et al., 2018). In the same way, our study indicates that the responses of growth processes to soil water deficit cannot be considered as independent from cycle duration. This can have large consequence in simulations. For example, for predicting the impact of climate change, one must consider the possible impact on such parameters if modifying the earliness of future genotypes. In ideotype studies, relationships between traits should be considered if idéotypes are not to be determined with sets of parameter values which cannot be achieved by breeding. Here, we descri- bed the structure of the genetic parameters of growth processes, and depending on the scientific question, it can indicate new formalisms taking into account such correlations or at least can suggest a minimum set of genetic parameters needed for considering the genetic diversity of whole-plant leaf area expansion and it is plasticity. [SOURCE: Page_144]

5.4      Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_144]

In this study, we evaluted the relationships and trade-offs between the com- ponents of shoot expansion and their sensitivities to water deficit and demons- trated that the genetic diversity of plasticity for shoot area expansion is mostly driven by that of tillering. Overall, this work can have large consequences in (i) ecophysiology, by describing the temporal dynamic patterns of shoot expansion [SOURCE: Page_144]

### 5.5. Material and methods                                                        143

[SOURCE: Page_145]

5.5     Material and methods [SOURCE: Page_145]

5.5.1   Genetic material [SOURCE: Page_145]

For this study, a set of 38 durum wheat genotypes (Triticum turgidum L. subsp. durum (Desf.) Husn. ; Supplementary Information Table S$_1$) and 47 bread wheat genotypes (T. aestivum L. ; Supplementary Information Table S$_2$) that represent a large genetic variability within each species was. [SOURCE: Page_145]

For bread wheat, 34 genotypes were selected from a genetic association panel of 200 lines sampled from 4,506 accessions in a worldwide collection (Balfourier et al., 2019) by minimizing linkage disequilibrium and by imposing agronomic constraints on phenology and plant height (Paux et al., 2022) and 10 European elite cultivars. These genotypes were selected to maximize the diversity of the responses of yield and spike number to water and nitrogen deficits (Supplemen- tary Information Fig. S12A). In addition, four spring wheat RILs from the Multi- Reference Nested Association Mapping population (MR-NAM) developed at the Queensland Alliance for Agriculture and Food Innovation (QAAFI ; Christopher et al., 2021) were added for their contrasted transpiration efficiency (SUNTOP_73 and SUNTOP_90 ; Christopher et al., 2018) and stay-green phenotype under ter- minal drought (SUNTOP_9 and SUNTOP_41 ; Fletcher, 2020), together with their three parental lines (Suntop, Drysdale, Dharwar-Dry). Three hybrid bread wheat genotypes (SY_117079, HYB15200646, and SY_117079) were also included in this study together with their three parents (Akamar, Oregrain, and Sublim). [SOURCE: Page_145]

For durum wheat, the genotypes were selected from a panel of 257 durum [SOURCE: Page_145]

144                        Chapitre 5. Tillering structures the genetic variability [SOURCE: Page_146]

wheat genotypes composed of 126 elite lines of worldwide origin registered bet- ween 1973 and 2015, 69 elite lines developed by the Council for Agricultural Re- search and Economics (CREA) and the University of Foggia, Italy, 61 lines ran- domly selected from 480 evolutionary pre-breeding lines derived from an open- pollinated population based on the intercrossing of about 650 accessions from wild subspecies and elite lines (David et al., 2014). The 38 durum wheat geno- types used in this study were selected from the 257 panel to maximize the diver- sity of the responses of grain yield and grain number per square to water and nitrogen deficit (Supplementary Information Fig. S12B). [SOURCE: Page_146]

Genotypes were separated into two groups. Group A (6 replicates by geno- type x water scenario) comprised four elite bread wheat cultivars and two elite durum wheat cultivars with contrasted behavior in the field (Supplementary In- formation Fig. S12). Two bread wheat cultivars that appeared as “sensitive” (Zit- nica and Couga), based their yield response under low-input in field experiments and two “tolerant” (Albatros and Torril), with contrasted response of spike num- ber per square meter. A “sensitive” durum wheat genotype (Nemesis), and a “to- lerant” durum wheat (Lahan), based on the maintenance of grain number under drought with contrasted response of grain number per square meter. From the six replicates of genotype x water scenario, three were harvested at anthesis. Ten watering scenarios were tested for group A. Only the four scenarios that were also tested in the group B are analyzed in this study. Group B (3 replicates by ge- notype x water scenario) comprised the other 43 bread wheat genotypes and 38 durum wheat genotypes (Nemesis was also in both groups). [SOURCE: Page_146]

5.5.2     Description of the PhenoArch plant phenotyping platform and [SOURCE: Page_146]

experimental design [SOURCE: Page_146]

The experiment was carried out in the PhenoArch platform, hosted in the Montpellier Plant Phenotyping Platforms (MP3, https://www6.montpellier.inra. fr/lepse/M3P). The setup and the functioning of PhenoArch has been described in details in Cabrera-Bosquet et al., 2016). Briefly, PhenoArch is a greenhouse [SOURCE: Page_146]

### 5.5. Material and methods                                                     145

equipped with conveyor belts carrying carts with one pot each (2,400 pots, ave- rage plant density of 12.5 plant m-2 ). Each day, plants are transported towards a watering station, allowing an accurate weighing of the pots and watering at tar- get soil water potential (SWP), and an imaging station equipped a top and a side RGB cameras. Plants are then moved back to the same positions and orientation, conserved throughout the whole experiment. Micrometeorological conditions at the plant level are monitored every minute at six positions in the glasshouse and average values are recorded every 15 min. The temperature of the meristema- tic zone of eight plants distributed in the glasshouse was also measured with copper-constantan thermocouples (0.2 mm diameter) vertically inserted between the sheaths of two leaves. Supplemental light was provided either during day- time when outside solar radiation was below 300 W m -2 or to extend the photo- period to 12 h. All information (Micrometeorological conditions, irrigation, plant weight, plant images, and manual plant measurements) were stored in the Pheno- typing hybrid Information System ( PHIS, Neveu et al., 2018). Air vapor pressure difference (VPD) was calculated at each time step as the difference in water vapor pressure between saturation at air temperature and the current vapor pressure in the air. Thermal time was calculated from hourly average meristem temperature using the beta function proposed by Wang et al. (2017), with minimum, optimum and maximum temperatures of 0, 27.5, and 40 °C, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_147]

The dry weight of soil in each pot was calculated at the beginning of the ex- periment from the fresh weight of soil and soil water content determined in soil samples (three soil samples were taken every 60 pots). Soil water content was then determined by automatically weighing the pots and removing the weight of plants (estimated daily from the plant imaging, see below) and the pot itself. A water-retention curve previously obtained in several experiments (Alvarez-Prado et al., 2018) by fitting predawn leaf water potential of drying plants measured with a Scholander-type pressure chamber (Soil Moisture Equipment Corp., Santa Barbara, USA) against soil water content with the Van Genucheten curve (Ge- nuchten and Th, 1980) was used to calculation of the mean SWP in each pot at [SOURCE: Page_147]

146                          Chapitre 5. Tillering structures the genetic variability [SOURCE: Page_148]

each weighing time. [SOURCE: Page_148]

5.5.3      Plant growth conditions and drought scenarios in the pheno- [SOURCE: Page_148]

typing platform [SOURCE: Page_148]

Dry seeds were sown on 28 November 2019 at 2 cm depth in 3 x 5 cm Jiffy pellets (Jiffy Group, Oslo, Norway) and placed in vernalization chamber were air temperature was controlled at 3.2±0.5 °C with a PPFD of 25 µmol m-2 s-1 and 16 h photoperiod. After 7 weeks in the vernalization chamber, plantlets were placed for 1 week under fluctuating conditions in the greenhouse of the platform (mean temperature 13.9±10°C). Homogenous plants (scored manually) from each genotype were then transplanted together with their Jiffy pellet into 5 L plastic pots (0.17 m diameter, 0.24 m high) filled with a 30 :70 (v :v) mixture of a clay and organic compost. From that date, plants stayed on the PhenoArch greenhouse under fluctuating conditions of air temperature, VPD and light (Supplementary Information Fig. S12) until final harvest at grain ripeness maturity on 12 May 2020. [SOURCE: Page_148]

The spatial design ordered 12 rows of genotypes of group A and 16 rows of genotypes from group B, with successively 2 rows of group A and 3 of group B (Supplementary Figure S$_3$). Each block of two successive rows with genotypes of group A corresponded to one replicate for group A. Genotypes from group B were fully randomized in the platform. [SOURCE: Page_148]

The whole experiment included 10 watering scenarios (for genotypes of group A) but only four, common to genotypes of group A and B, were analyzed in this study : [SOURCE: Page_148]

— Scenario 1 (S1) : Well-watered conditions (control). SWP was maintained at -0.02 MPa during the whole plant growth cycle ; — Scenario 2 (S2) : Late and short water deficit, a 3-weeks period of wa- ter deficit between the beginning of stem elongation (average of all ge- notypes) until anthesis of the earliest genotypes. Water was withheld on [SOURCE: Page_148]

### 5.5. Material and methods                                                         147

12 February 2020 until SWP decreaed to - 0.54 MPa, after wich SWP was maintained at that value. At the end of the 3-weeks period of water deficit (6 March 2020), pots were rewatered over a 5 d period during which SWP as gradually increased to 72, 86, 100, 114, and 124 %, and then maintained at -0.02 MPa until final harvest. — Scenario 3 (S$_3$) : Early and short water deficit, a 3-weeks period of water deficit starting at the beginning of tillering when the plants had three to four visible leaves depending on the genotype. Water was withheld on 3 February 2020 until SWP decreaed to - 0.54 MPa. At the end of the 3-weeks period (28 Februray 2020), pots were slowly rewatered as in S$_2$. — Scenario 4 (S$_4$) : Early and long water deficit, a 31-d period of water deficit starting at the beginning of tillering (3 February 2020) until anthesis of the earliest genotypes (6 March 2020). [SOURCE: Page_149]

5.5.4    Automatic measurements of whole plant shoot area [SOURCE: Page_149]

An analysis pipeline was used to calculate whole plant leaf area (shoot SA) and fresh weight from RGB images (2056 × 2454 dpi) taken from 3 angle views (2 side views and one top view) every 2 to 3 d for each plant in the platform. Briefly, plant pixels from each image were segmented from those of the background and projected plant area were then calculated using calibration curves obtained pre- viously using reference objects to convert pixels into projected plant green area ( ;Alvarez-Prado et al., 2018). Multiple-linear regression models (previously pa- rametrized from destructive measurements) were then used to calculate whole plant shoot area and plant fresh weight. [SOURCE: Page_149]

5.5.5    Manual plant measurements [SOURCE: Page_149]

The number of leaves on the main-stem (LN) and green tillers per plant (TN) of each plant were counted twice a week from the beginning of tillering to anthe- sis. The former was calculated as (Haun, 1973) : [SOURCE: Page_149]

148                            Chapitre 5. Tillering structures the genetic variability [SOURCE: Page_150]

l LN = n +                                       (5.1) L [SOURCE: Page_150]

where n is the number of mature leaves (with the ligule appeared), l is the exposed length of leaf n+1 at the time of measurement, and L is the final length of the blade of leaf n+1. The exposed length of a leaf was measured with a ruler as the distance from leaf tip to the upper collar of the sheath tube. [SOURCE: Page_150]

Final (mature) leaf length and width of each newly mature leaf on the main stem were measured with a ruler. The surface area of the second leave on the main stem (LA$_2$), a proxy of plant early vigor (Rebetzke, 1999), was calculated as its length times its wight times an allometric factor or 0.75 (Dornbusch et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_150]

5.5.6     Calculation of rates and sensitivities of soil water deficit [SOURCE: Page_150]

Thermal time was calculated from the appearance of leaf 3 of the main stem, which for all genotypes occurred after the transfer of the plants on the PhenoArch conveyor. Thermal time was calculated from hourly average meristem tempera- ture using a nonlinear beta function (Wang and Engel, 1998), with minimum, optimum and maximum temperatures of 0, 27.5, and 40 °C, respectively (Wang et al. 2017 ; Parent et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_150]

We analyzed four processes (Table 5.1), the rate of whole plant leaf area ex- pansion (SER) and the three underlying processes, the rates of tiller appearance (TiR), leaf appearance (LAR), and individual leaf elongation on the main stem (LER). For each process, several variables were compared between genotypes (Table 5.1) : (i) the time course of the corresponding variable as a function of thermal time ; (ii) its maximum value ; (iii) the time course of the related rate as a function of thermal time ; (iv) its maximum (for SER, TiR, and LER) or average value (for LAR) ; and (v) the relative sensitivity of the rate to soil water deficit (i.e. the slope of the relationship between relative rate and SWP). [SOURCE: Page_150]

### 5.5. Material and methods                                                         149

SA and TN versus thermal time after leaf-3 appearance were fitted to a cubic smoothing spline function equation using the smooth.spline() function of the R package stats with spar parameter set at 0.6 and 0.4, respectively. LN versus ther- mal time was fitted to a linear model forced to 3 at 0°Cd. For the control treatment (scenario 1), the spline function or linear model was fitted to the data of all the plants of each genotype (n = 3 to 6 replicates for group B and A, respectively), while for the water deficit scenario, the data for each plant were fitted separately. The rate of each process was then calculated as the first derivative of the fitted spline or linear regression. We used the R function predict() to calculate SER and TiR. We calculated LAR as the slope of the linear regression. LER was calculated as CCL times LARmean. Finally, for each process and each plant subjected to wa- ter deficit, relative rates were calculated by dividing the absolute rates by those of well-watered plants (S$_1$ ; average of all plants of the same genotype) at the same thermal time. [SOURCE: Page_151]

For each process and genotype, we defined a time period during which the rate of the process for the well-watered planted was high enough to accurately calculate SWP sensitivity. This period was defined from the time the rate was at 30% of its maximum value and either the time it had decreased to 20% of its maximum value or the date of the first rewatering, whichever occurred first (Fig. 5.1BD). For each genotype, the sensitivity of each process to soil water deficit was calculated during this period as the slope of the relationship between relative rate versus SWP forced to 1 at SWP is null, using data for the three water deficit scenarios (Fig. 5.1D). [SOURCE: Page_151]

5.5.7   Data analyses [SOURCE: Page_151]

All data analyses were performed with the R statistical software program R (R Core Team 2018). [SOURCE: Page_151]

## CHAPITRE 6

How can wheat growth traits and their sensitivity to water [SOURCE: Page_159]

deficit measured in controlled phenotyping platform [SOURCE: Page_159]

field ? [SOURCE: Page_159]

6.1     Introduction [SOURCE: Page_159]

In the 2000s, the advent of efficient, inexpensive and rapid genomic and gene- tic methods shifted the bottleneck of genetic / breeding pipelines to phenotyping (Berger et al., 2010 ; Furbank and Tester, 2011 ; Yang et al., 2020). Indeed, at that time, there was no efficient method to measure physiological traits on hundreds of genotypes, which therefore required large experimental and financial efforts. [SOURCE: Page_159]

In the 2010s, high-throughput phenotyping platforms (Fiorani and Schurr, 2013 ; Janni and Pieruschka, 2022) have been developed and thus multiplied, in all their diversity of temporal and spatial scales, for different types of traits, from [SOURCE: Page_159]

157 [SOURCE: Page_159]

Chapitre 6. How can phenotyping platform inform on trait of genotypes in the 158                                                                     field ? metabolites (e.g. HitMe), to shoot (e.g. M3P PhenoArch, Cabrera-Bosquet et al., 2016 ; M3P PhenoPsis, Granier et al., 2006 and root growth (4PMI ; Jeudy et al., 2016 under controlled conditions , or in the semi-controlled field (e.g. Pheno- Field ; Beauchêne et al., 2019 ; FIP, Kirchgessner et al., 2017). In this landscape, one of the roles of imaging platforms under controlled conditions, such as the M3P PhenoArch platform used in Chapter 5, is to allow the phenotyping of a large number of plants under well characterized and controlled environmental condi- tion, using consistent and reproducible methods between experiments. However, to date, studies using these platforms have mainly focused on the development of new tools and methods but have rarely been used to study specific biologi- cal questions related to crop improvement (Prado et al., 2018 ; Perez et al., 2019). The result is that such platforms, although very expensive and widely developed recently in several laboratories, have not found their undisputed place, as other types of platforms could. [SOURCE: Page_160]

One of the reasons is the mistrust towards platforms under controlled condi- tions, i.e. plants are grown in pots at a low density and under environmental conditions that are very different from those in the field. For example, in the Phe- noArch platform, the density of pots is 12 plants per square meter, which is close to the field density for maize but is far from the 100-400 plants per m^2 of a wheat field. [SOURCE: Page_160]

In parallel, field phenotyping is experiencing an explosion of new imagery technology (e.g. RGB, multi- and hyper-spectral imagery, LIDAR, NDVI) and analytics methods and pipelines (e.g. deep learning) and platforms (mobile rai- nout shelters, meteorological and soil sensor networks), and highly-funded inter- national projects are also seeing the future of phenotyping around remote sensing (Ghosal et al., 2018 ; Machwitz et al., 2021 ; Fu et al., 2022). Field phenotyping plat- forms provide crop growth conditions that are closer to agronomic conditions, but they do not allow a total control of environmental conditions such as water deficit scenario and in the field of crops are usually phenotyped with revisit time of several days, while plant responded to environmental conditions with a time [SOURCE: Page_160]

### 6.2. Material and Methods                                                         159

scale of minutes to hours (Tardieu and Parent, 2017). Therefore, field and control- led environment phenotyping platforms should not be considered as alternative tools and methods. We should rather take advantage of their complementarity to better understand and characterize the diversity of physiological and architectu- ral traits associated with plant growth potential and adaptation to environmental constraints, in order to ultimately predict their performance in the diversity of environmental scenarios encountered in the field. [SOURCE: Page_161]

In this Chapter, I therefore analyzed the bread wheat and durum wheat pa- nels constituted in Chapter 4 that maximize the variability of yield sensitivity and its components to nitrogen and water limited conditions ; in a high through- put phenotyping platform under controlled conditions (Chapter 5) and in a field platform equipped with phenotyping tools (PhenoMobile) to measure shoot ex- pansion (green area index, NDVI). I analyzed the correlations between dynamic variables related to shoot expansion and grain yield and their sensitivity to water deficit in the platform and in the field, and, more broadly, how dynamic variables of plant growth measured in platform are related to more integrated variables (grain yield and its components) in the field. This study demonstrates that the use of a phenotyping platform under controlled conditions can capture the gene- tic variability of growth rates measured in the field, as well as part of the variance that explains yield formation. [SOURCE: Page_161]

6.2     Material and Methods [SOURCE: Page_161]

6.2.1   Genetic Material [SOURCE: Page_161]

In this chapter, we analyzed 33 durum wheat and 44 bread wheat cultivars that were phenotyped both under controlled conditions (Chapter 5) and in the field (Chapter 4). These genotypes were selected (see Chapter 4) to maximize the diversity of the response of grain yield and yield components to low-input (water and nitrogen) conditions. Only the genotypes grown both in the platform and in the field were analyzed in this chapter (Supplementary Information Table S9). [SOURCE: Page_161]

Chapitre 6. How can phenotyping platform inform on trait of genotypes in the 160                                                                     field ? 6.2.2    Field trials [SOURCE: Page_162]

Two field trials conducted at ARVALIS experimental station at Gréoux-les- bains, France (43°44’ N, 5°51’ E, 293 m a.s.l.) during the 2017-2018 (trial ARVgre^2018) and 2018-2019 (trial ARVgre^2018) for bread and durum wheat, respectively. The growth conditions during these trials are summarized in Table 6.1. Crops were sown on 29 October 2017 and 20 November 2018 at a density of 300 seeds m-2 . Plots consisted in 11 rows with an inter-row spacing of 0.175 m and a surface area of 11.9 m-2 . In both trial, two environmental conditions were tested (Table 6.1), a control condition with optimal water and nitrogen supply (named high- input, HI, hereafter) and another condition with low nitrogen and water supply for bread wheat and with low nitrogen for durum wheat (named low-input, LI, hereafter). Each trial used a split-plot design in which the LI and HI treatments were randomized on main plots, cultivar was randomized on the sub-plots and each treatment was replicated two times. [SOURCE: Page_162]

Nitrogen fertilizer applications in the HI treatments were intended to repli- cate commercial practice. The growth stages and application rates were calcula- ted based on provisional N balance associated with expected grain yields using the N sheet balance method described in (Rémy and Hébert, 1977). For the bread wheat trial, the LI treatment N fertilizer was reduced by 72% compared with the HI treatment. Soil water content at 30-cm and 60-cm below the soil surface was monitored throughout the growing season using tensiometric probes. In the HI treatment with low water supply, irrigation was applied to ensure that water supply did not limit plant growth. In the bread wheat trial, the LI treatment recei- ved 15 mm of irrigaton (compared wth 200 mm in the HI treatment). The durum [SOURCE: Page_162]

wheat trial was rainfed. All other crop inputs including weed, disease and pest control, and potassium, phosphate, and sulfur fertilizers, were applied at levels to prevent non-N nutrients or weeds, diseases and pests from limiting grain yield. [SOURCE: Page_163]

Heading date, grain yield, average grain dry mass (GrainW, calculated from a sample of 1200 grains) and spike number per square meter (SpikeNb) was deter- mined (Table 6.2). Average grain number per spike (GrainSpike) was calculated as GrainNb divided by SpikeNB and grain number per square meter (GrainNb) was calculated as Yield divided by GrainNb. [SOURCE: Page_163]

Normalized difference vegetation index (NDVI), green area index (GAI), and canopy height (CH) were determined with a self-guided rover equipped with RGB and multi-spectral cameras mounted at 0° and 45° zenithal view angles (PHENOMOBILE® V$_1$ 1 ; Madec et al., 2017) using the procedures described by Madec et al.(2017). NDVI values measured at the two view angels were averaged. In both trials, measurements were taken every about 500°Cd and every 100°Cd [SOURCE: Page_163]

Chapitre 6. How can phenotyping platform inform on trait of genotypes in the 162                                                                     field ? between 1000°Cd and 1500°Cd and grain ripeness maturity. The rates of change of NDVI and GAI were calculated as the first derivative of a cubic smoothing spline fitted to the absolute values versus thermal time after sowing (Absolute value, Fig. 6.1AB ; derivative, Fig. 6.1DE). The best spar parameter of the R func- tion smooth.spline() was first estimated for each genotype / treatment combina- tion, and the median value (0.5 and 0.6 for GAI and NDVI) was used for all the plants. For CH, a segmented linear model was fitted to the absolute values ver- sus thermal time after anthesis (Fig.1C) and the average rate of canopy height expansion (CHmean) was calculated as the slope of the second linear segment. [SOURCE: Page_164]

6.2.3   Experiment in the phenotyping platform PhenoArch [SOURCE: Page_164]

The experiment conducted in the plant phenotyping platform PhenoArch described in Chapter 5 was used in this chapter. All growth traits calculated in Chapter 5 were analyzed, namely the maximum value, maximum or average rate and sensitivity to water deficit of shoot area, individual leaf length, leaf appea- rance, and tiller appearance (Table 3). The calculation procedures are described in Chapter 5. [SOURCE: Page_164]

In addition, in this chapter we analyzed the elongation of the internodes of the main stem. The length of all internodes on the main stem was measured with a ruler at ripeness maturity. The cumulative length of internodes on the main stem (CIL) was calculated as the sum of individual internode length. The period during which each internode was growing was calculated afterwards based on leaf appearance, by considering that the leaf of phytomer n expand at the same time as the internode of phytomer n-1 (Evers et al., 2005). The rate of internode expansion (IER) was calculated during each period as CIL times LARmean. The cumulative internode length (CIL), the maximum internode elongation rate (IER- max), and the sensitivity of internode elongation rate to soil water deficit (IER) were calculated has described in Chapter 5 for leaf expansion traits. [SOURCE: Page_164]

Chapitre 6. How can phenotyping platform inform on trait of genotypes in the 164                                                                     field ? 6.2.4   Data analysis [SOURCE: Page_166]

For all variables, the sensitivity to low input conditions was calculated as the normalized difference : [SOURCE: Page_166]

(high input − low input) Sensitivity o f variable =                                      (6.1) high input [SOURCE: Page_166]

Variables measured or calculated in the field and in the platform experiment are described in Table 6.2 and 6.3, respectively. All data analyses were carried out using the statistical software program R (R Core Team, 2018). Principal com- ponent analyses (PCAs) were performed using the PCA() function of the R pa- ckage FactorMineR (Lê et al., 2008). A hierarchical clustering analysis on principal components (HCPC) was performed using the pvclust() function of the R pa- ckage pv.clust (Suzuki and Shimodaira, 2006 ; Suzuki et al., 2019). One thousand bootstrap samplings were performed to calculate unbiased (AU) P-value. [SOURCE: Page_166]

6.3     Results [SOURCE: Page_166]

6.3.1   Comparison of dynamic growth traits in the field and in control- [SOURCE: Page_166]

led environment [SOURCE: Page_166]

Together with yield components, we calculated metrics of plant growth that reflect the growth dynamics and its sensitivity to soil limiting conditions, both on the platform and in the field, and for both bread wheat and durum wheat genotypes. Results for the platform experiment are presented in chapter 5. [SOURCE: Page_166]

In the field, the time course of canopy height under both high- and low-input conditions for both bread (Fig. 6.1C) and durum (Supplementary Information Fig. S22) wheat genotypes was well described by a segmented linear model, with a period during which canopy height increased linearly with thermal time that corresponded to period of internode expansion. As illustrated in Fig. 6.1C for the bread wheat cultivar 2109.36C, both CHR and CHmax were reducd under LI [SOURCE: Page_166]

### 6.3. Results                                                                     165

conditions compared with HI conditions. Similar results were obtained for du- rum wheat (Supplementary Information Fig. S22). The genotypic values of the variables measured in the field are summarized in Supplementary Information Table S$_9$. [SOURCE: Page_167]

In both high- and low-input conditions, GAI (Fig. 6.1A) and NDVI (Fig. 6.1B) showed a period with an almost-linear increase, followed by a period of decrease due to leaf senescence. Fitting spline curves to observed GAI and NDVI data al- lowed us to calculate their rate of change as a function of thermal time (Fig. 6.1D) and NDVI (Fig. 6.1E). Both types of growth indicators showed that canopy area expansion was reduced under LI conditions compared to HI conditions. In a simi- lar way to that calculated in the platform, genotypes were compared based on (i) the maximum value of the integrated variable ; (ii) the maximum rate of change ; and (iii) the sensitivity of the rates to limiting conditions (calculated as the nor- malized difference between LI and HI). Again, similar results were obtained in durum wheat (SI Fig. S$_1$). [SOURCE: Page_167]

6.3.2    Hierarchical clustering discriminated maximal growth traits from [SOURCE: Page_167]

growth sensitivity traits, whether in the platform or in the field [SOURCE: Page_167]

To approach and better understand the relationship between dynamic va- riables related to growth, their sensitivities, together with other variables and sensitivities related to grain yield components, we performed a PCA and a HCPC on all traits measured in the platform and in the field. Results for the two species were largely similar, thus below I only present the results for bread wheat. Results for durum wheat are shown in Supplementary information Figure S23 . Similar PCA and HCPC analyses were performed including the field trials presented in Chapiter 3, in which variables related to growth were not measured. These re- sults do not affect our conclusions regarding yield components (see below). They are also presented as Supplementary Information Fig. S23 but are not described in the result section. [SOURCE: Page_167]

Chapitre 6. How can phenotyping platform inform on trait of genotypes in the 166                                                                     field ? The number of traits analyzed (40) in the PCA does not allow for direct conclu- sions (Fig. 6.2AB). However, several indications can be identified at a broader level. First, this analysis did not discriminate field variables from platform va- riables. Second, it did not discriminate sensitivity variables from rate or yield component variables. Together, these results indicate that correlations should be found between such traits. [SOURCE: Page_168]

Hierarchical classification of these variables allowed for a more detailed ap- proach to the relationships between these variables. (Fig. 6.2C). In particular, the first level of clustering separated a group of variables in which appeared most of the variables related to maximum growth in the field and in the platform (e.g. TIRmax and SERmax in the platform and GAIRmax in the field), and the va- riables related to the sensitivity of these variables. Note that these results were even clearer for durum wheat and for bread wheat (SI Fig. S23). The approxi- mately unbiased P-values were high, especially for a small group (AU = 95, Fig. 6.2C) that included the variables related to tillering (TIRmax, TNmax) and whole plant growth (SERmax, SAmax) measured in the platform, and green area index (GAImax, GAIRmax) measured in the field. [SOURCE: Page_168]

These relationships, observed here on a large scale, could be studied side by side, by biplots (Fig. 6.3) and Pearson’s corelations. In particular, the maximum rate of increase of GAI in the field (GAIRmax) was significantly correlated with the maximum number of appeared tiller per plant (TiRmax) and total leaf area (SERmax) measured in platform. It was less clear regarding sensitivity traits. In- deed, while significant relationships were found between sensitivity traits mea- sured in platform (see Chapter 5) or in the field (SNDVI and SGAI , P < 0.01 ; Fig. 6.4), no significant correlation were found between sensitivity traits in the field and in the platform. [SOURCE: Page_168]

6.3.3    Sensitivities of both growth traits in the platform and in the [SOURCE: Page_172]

field correlated to that of grain number in the field [SOURCE: Page_172]

The above paragraph showed that a large cluster included variables related to maximum growth both in the field and in the platform. However, these va- riables were related to only a few components of grain yield in the field. This was confirmed by the pairwise correlations (Supplementary Information Fig. S24). In contrast, the hierarchical clustering (Fig. 6.2) suggested relationships between grain yield and yield components measured in the field and growth sensitivi- ties, whether measured in the field or in the platform (Fig. 6.2). The large group that included most of the growth sensitivity traits in the field (SGAI , SNDVI ) or in the platform (STiR , SSER ), also inclided most of the variables related to grain yield component sensitivities. [SOURCE: Page_172]

A second level of clustering discriminated a group of variables (AU = 80) with the number of grains (GrainSpike and GrainNb), their sensitivity (SGrainSpike and SGrainNb ), as well as SGAI and SNDVI . Most of these relationships were significant [SOURCE: Page_172]

in the pairwise correlations (Fig. 6.5). A second group (AU = 70) included Yield and SYield , as well as STiR and SSER in the platform. ### 6.4. Discussion                                                                    173

6.4     Discussion [SOURCE: Page_175]

6.4.1    Growth traits and their sensitivities to water deficit measured [SOURCE: Page_175]

in platform can provide inform on growth potential in the field [SOURCE: Page_175]

Field phenotyping faces several limitations for traits related to plant sensiti- vity to soil water deficit (Berger et al., 2010) : (i) the random and experimentally risky aspect of water deficit that may or may not occur, even several years in a row under rainfed conditions and in low latitudes ; (ii) its very dynamic, non-stable aspect, which complicates the analyses, especially for plants with different phe- nology, with key stages of development at very different times, under different water status ; (iii) The difficulty to quantify this water deficit, which is by essence is very different according to soil depth, and depending on the associated root and soil/root conductance ; (iv) The interaction of water deficit with other abio- tic stresses, in particular thermal stress, due to the concomitance of drought and high temperature periods and the fact that a decrease in transpiration also affects the temperature of the plant (Parent et al., 2017). For all these reasons, trait-based selection has mainly focused on constitutive traits, easily phenotyped and giving advantages in most water-deficit scenarios. It is therefore reasonable to focus the phenotyping of water-deficit response traits in controlled condition platforms. However, such platforms do not reflect agronomic conditions and therefore the output parameters may not be extrapolated to the field. [SOURCE: Page_175]

In this study, we showed instead that the underlying parameters of growth were strongly correlated in the platform and in the field. These correlations im- ply that a genetic analysis based on growth parameters should result in genetic markers of interest for field conditions. In addition, parameters related to indivi- dual leaf growth and tillering (see Chapter 5) are in the process of being automa- tically phenotyped in the PhenoArch platform. Knowing that these parameters can be easily linked to genotypic parameters of crop models supports the idea that these highly controlled platforms can be of interest in genetic trait impact studies and for model-assisted breeding. Overall, these results can support the [SOURCE: Page_175]

Chapitre 6. How can phenotyping platform inform on trait of genotypes in the 174                                                                     field ? idea that highly controlled platforms can have a place in academic research and pre-breeding pipelines, and that such place does not depend on the development (or not) of field phenotyping but rather on specific focus on adaptive traits such as drought response traits. [SOURCE: Page_176]

6.4.2    Is the sensitivity of shoot growth in the platform related to that [SOURCE: Page_176]

of grain number in the field [SOURCE: Page_176]

Much has been written about the debate on yield control under water deficit, whether its limitation by carbon sinks or sources and/or the most important un- derlying component, either grain number or grain filling (Sinclair and Jamieson, 2008). I have already discussed this aspect in Chapter 4, concluding that there is, however, the beginnings of a compromise in the literature, targeting grain num- ber as the primary control on yield. Some of these conclusions of Chapter 4 are again found here. Here, in addition to this robustness in contrasted fields (Chap- ter 4) we also show a very little G x E interactions on grain dry mass between field and platform (very close in clustering, Fig. 6.2). [SOURCE: Page_176]

Another major result showed the relationship between the sensitivity of growth measured in the platform and the sensitivity of the number of grains in the field. Such a relationship has already been observed in maize for the sensitivity of in- dividual leaf elongation to soil water deficit measured in the PhenoDyn platform (Welcker et al., 2011) and the sensitivity of the number of grain number per square meter in the field, calculated in the same way as in our study (Dignat et al., 2013). In maize, this relationship was interpreted through common determinisms for the sensitivities of different growth processes, and in particular leaf growth and silk growth. Silk growth itself determines grain number in maize (Oury et al., 2016 ; Turc et al., 2016). Here, in wheat, the putative mechanism involved is ne- cessarily different. However, the relationship between growth and grain number during a pre-flowering sensitivity period is a type of formalism that is widely used in crop models, e.g. Sirius (Martre et al., 2006 ; He et al., 2012). Here, the fact that this relationship is found on sensitivities (and not absolute values) indicates [SOURCE: Page_176]

that such sensitivities parameters, common to growth and grain number, could be used in new formalisms for grain number calculation, simplifying the pheno- typing of hundreds of varieties and contributing to the parsimony of genotypic parameters in crop models. [SOURCE: Page_177]

## CHAPITRE 7

Résumé des recherches [SOURCE: Page_183]

Dans le Chapitre 3, j’ai étudié la structure de la variabilité génétique des ré- ponses de la croissance foliaire et de la transpiration à la température et au VPD chez 12 espèces et sous-espèces apparentées au blé. Pour cela, j’ai défini des for- malismes de réponses et quantifié ces réponses (valeurs de paramètres) en phé- notypant cinq variétés par espèce/sous espèce. Mes résultats indiquent que ces traits de réponses sont bien conservés à l’échelle intra-spécifique, en particulier pour les réponses à la température, mais qu’il existe une diversité à l’échelle inter- spécifique. Cette diversité est largement structurée par les liens phylogénétiques entre les espèces et sous espèces, leur niveau de ploïdie et dans une moindre me- sure par la domestication. Enfin , outre le fait de proposer des formalismes et des valeurs de paramètres utiles en modélisation des cultures, mon étude a permis d’identifier des sources potentielles d’amélioration variétale qui résident actuel- lement dans les espèces apparentées au blé. [SOURCE: Page_183]

Dans le Chapitre 4, j’ai tout d’abord étudié les contraintes/relations entre composantes du rendement (définissant l’espace phénotypique), comment ces re- [SOURCE: Page_183]

181 [SOURCE: Page_183]

182                                               Chapitre 7. Résumé des recherches [SOURCE: Page_184]

lations sont elles-même affectées par des contraintes hydriques et azotées, et en- fin, j’ai cherché à sélectionner un sous panel qui respecterait l’espace des possibles observés dans le panel de départ. Plus précisément, j’ai montré ici que l’ensemble des corrélations entre le rendement, ses composantes et leurs sensibilités, définis- sait un espace phénotypique très contraint. Ces relations étant similaires chez le blé dur et le blé tendre, mes résultats indiquent qu’une hypothèse de contraintes génétiques fortes (proximité, lien entre régions génomiques) pour expliquer les liens entre variables est moins probable que l’hypothèse de contraintes évolutives et agronomiques communes entre les deux espèces. Notre sélection génotypique a respecté cet espace phénotypique, dans le sens où elle a conservé toutes ses composantes, les relations entre variables et la variabilité génétique conduisant à ces relations. Les deux sous panel résultants de cette étape sont ceux utilisés pendant le reste de ma thèse. [SOURCE: Page_184]

Dans le Chapitre 5, j’ai cherché à déterminer les compromis entre les compo- santes de l’expansion foliaire à l’échelle de la plante entière et leurs sensibilités au déficit hydrique. J’ai pu démontrer que la diversité observée de croissance foliaire était largement la résultante de celle du tallage, en tout cas plus que de celle de la vitesse d’émission des feuilles ou l’élongation de feuilles individuelles. De même, j’ai montré que la plasticité de la surface foliaire était principalement déterminée par celle du tallage. Dans l’ensemble, ce travail peut avoir des conséquences im- portantes en (i) écophysiologie, en décrivant la dynamique de croissance foliaire, ainsi que les parts respectives de ses sous-composantes, leurs plasticité et leurs relations réciproques ; (ii) pour la sélection, en identifiant les traits cibles sous dé- ficit hydrique ainsi que la variabilité génétique associée ; et (iii) en modélisation, en décrivant des formalisme simples de réponse, ainsi que la structure des para- mètres génétiques associés. [SOURCE: Page_184]

Dans le Chapitre 6, j’ai questionné l’intérêt de la démarche, en explorant les liens entre ces variables mesurées en plateformes, les variables de croissance me- surées au champ, et jusqu’aux composantes du rendement. Les résultats concer- nant les paramètres de croissance étaient positifs, au sens qu’ils étaient corré- [SOURCE: Page_184]

183 [SOURCE: Page_185]

lés entre la plateforme et le champ. Ces corrélations impliquent par exemple qu’une analyse génétique basée sur des paramètres de croissance mesurés en pla- teforme devrait aboutir à des marqueurs génétiques d’intérêt pour les conditions au champ. Le fait que ces paramètres puissent être facilement liés aux paramètres génotypiques des modèles de culture renforce l’idée que ces plateformes de phé- notypage peuvent être intéressantes pour les études d’impact des caractères gé- nétiques et pour la sélection assistée par modèle. Un autre résultat important est l’existence d’une relation entre la sensibilité de la croissance mesurée dans la pla- teforme et la sensibilité du nombre de grains au champ. Le fait que cette relation soit trouvée sur les sensibilités (et non sur les valeurs absolues) indique que de tels paramètres de sensibilité, communs à la croissance et au nombre de grains, pourraient être utilisés dans de nouveaux formalismes pour le calcul du nombre de grains, simplifiant le phénotypage de centaines de variétés et contribuant à la diminution des paramètres génotypiques dans les modèles de culture. [SOURCE: Page_185]
